### Dedicatory Epistle. 5

...I can auspiciously begin: and that especially because this work directs all its strength <note>original: *neruos totos*; literally "all its nerves" or "all its sinews," meaning the full force of the argument</note> toward this end, so that the medical and physical arts <note>In this period, "physics" or the "physical arts" referred to the study of natural science and the human body.</note>—which have relied on the false foundations of the pagans <note>original: *Aethnicorum*; the author is referring to ancient Greek and Roman authorities like Galen and Aristotle, whose "heathen" theories the Paracelsians sought to replace with Christian and alchemical truths.</note>—may return to their very origins of truth. Indeed, it seems to matter quite a bit to look ahead and see to which medicines and to which physicians people—and especially Kings and Princes—entrust their lives. For those very drugs which have been in use until now are unhealthy and prone to decay, such that even if they are stored for a short time without being consumed, they grow "sick" themselves; thus they introduce diseases rather than driving them away as they ought. Since it is necessary that like is generated from like—disease from disease, just as health comes from health and corruption from corruption. Therefore, our teacher <term>Paracelsus<note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist who famously challenged traditional medical dogma.</note></term> teaches, by a far superior method, how to treat the parts of life that are loosened and discordant through decay by using medicines that have been stripped of all corruptibility through the <term>spagyric art<note>The alchemical process of "separating and recombining." It involves breaking a substance down into its essential elements (sulfur, mercury, and salt) to purify them before reuniting them into a potent medicine.</note></term>.

# 8 Dedicatory Letter

...to bring [them] in various ways to the proportion of **Unity** and health, through a certain sympathy: not from the schools of the unfaithful Heathens <note>original: *Aethnicorum* — referring to non-Christian or pagan philosophers like Aristotle or Galen, whose medical theories the author is rejecting</note>, but from the one true fountain of all sciences and the center of **Truth**, of which the Heathens and their disciples were utterly ignorant. 

As will be revealed more extensively in my *Monarchy* <note>The author refers to his own book, likely titled *Monarchia*, which would explain his specific system of natural philosophy</note>: in which the true foundations of the medical art will be made known through the most solid arguments, and those medical principles which **Paracelsus** <note>Theophrastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a famous and controversial Swiss physician who rejected traditional Greek medicine in favor of alchemy and chemistry</note> brought to light will be established upon an unshakable foundation. 

To settle the matter briefly: there is no art and no wisdom which has not flowed from the center of **Unity** and **Truth**, just as a line flows from a single point. 

Therefore, I pray you, Most Illustrious Prince, receive from the hands of your most humble servant these things which until now were unheard of with such clarity; protect them with your patronage against those giant-like men who are enemies of the truth, and defend them as you are able from injury and envy. Meanwhile, may Your Highness fare well in Jesus Christ, and live for all eternity.

<summary>This page concludes a dedicatory preface to a Prince, arguing that true medicine stems from divine "Unity" rather than pagan philosophy. The author promises to defend the radical medical principles of Paracelsus and asks for the Prince's protection against critics.</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsus, medical philosophy, Unity and Truth, patronage, alchemy, Heathen schools, Monarchy of Nature</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page begins the treatise "Aurora Philosophorum," attributed to the influential Renaissance physician and alchemist Paracelsus. It establishes a mythical lineage for alchemical knowledge, tracing it back to Adam and the antediluvian world.</meta>

# AURORA
->The Aurora of the Philosophers by Doctor<-
->**THEOPHRASTUS PARACELSUS**<-

## Chapter One.

### On the Origin of the Philosophers’ Stone.

**A**dam was the first inventor of the arts because he possessed the knowledge of all things, both after the Fall <note>The biblical exile from the Garden of Eden</note> just as he did before the Fall. From this knowledge, he foresaw that the world would pass through water <note>original: *per aquam transiturum*; a prophecy of the Great Flood</note>. For this very reason, it came to pass that his successors erected two stone tablets upon which they carved all the natural arts in <term>hieroglyphic characters</term> <note>In the 16th century, "hieroglyphs" referred generally to any sacred, symbolic, or encoded emblems used to hide secrets from the uninitiated</note>, so that this prophecy might be made known to posterity and be observed, and that provision should be made in times of danger.

After the Flood had passed, Noah eventually found one of these tablets in Armenia at the foot of Mount Ararat. On this tablet were described the courses of the upper firmament <note>the celestial spheres or heavens</note> and the lower globe <note>the Earth</note>, as well as the paths of the planets. At length, this universal knowledge was broken apart into various pieces and its power was diminished. This separation resulted in one person becoming an astronomer, another a mage, another a <term>cabalist</term> <note>a practitioner of Kabbalah, a form of Jewish mysticism that Paracelsus integrated into his natural philosophy</note>, and a fourth an alchemist. That Vulcan-like Abraham <note>original: *Vulcanicus ille Abraham*; Paracelsus often links the biblical patriarchs to "Vulcan," the god of fire, to suggest they possessed the secrets of the forge and alchemy</note>, Tubal-cain, who was a supreme astrologer and mathematician, brought this knowledge out of Egypt into the land of Canaan.

<summary>Paracelsus claims that alchemical and astronomical knowledge originated with Adam and was preserved from the Great Flood on stone tablets found by Noah. This "universal science" was later fragmented into separate disciplines like alchemy, astrology, and magic.</summary>

<keywords>Adam, Noah, Mount Ararat, Hieroglyphics, Alchemy, Astrology, Abraham, Tubal-cain, Origins of Science</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the origins of ancient wisdom, moving from the influence of the Egyptians to the biblical patriarchs and prophets who practiced these secret arts.</meta>

->PHILOSOPHY. 9<-

it led to the height where the Egyptians rose so high that the wisdom of this same matter passed from them even to other nations. Since indeed the Patriarch Jacob’s breeding of sheep with various colors <note>Refers to Genesis 30:37-39, where Jacob used peeled branches to influence the markings of his flocks; the author classifies this as a "branch of magic."</note> was made a branch of magic. For always the Chaldeans, Hebrews, Persians, and Egyptians, alongside Theology, proposed these **liberal arts** to their Nobles and Priests as the highest philosophy to be learned. It happened no differently in the times of Moses, when both the Priests and the physicians themselves were chosen from among the magi <note>The author uses "magi" here to mean wise men or practitioners of sacred sciences.</note>: the latter indeed in their judgment concerning health, especially in the recognition of leprosy. Moses also, by the care and expense of Pharaoh’s daughter, was instructed in the schools of the Egyptians so that he might excel in their wisdom. In no other way did Daniel from his early youth suckle the doctrine of the Chaldeans, so that he might turn out to be a <term>cabalist</term> <note>A practitioner of Kabbalah, a system of Jewish mysticism. The author suggests Daniel used these techniques to interpret divine signs.</note>. His divine prophecies bear witness to this, as do his explanations of those words: *Numbered, Numbered, Weighed, Divided* <note>original: "Mene Mene Tecelphares." These are the famous words written by a ghostly hand on the wall during Belshazzar’s feast in Daniel 5.</note>. These words must be understood through the prophetic and cabalistic art. The tradition of this <term>cabalistic</term> art was very familiar and most frequently used among Moses and the prophets. The prophet Elias <note>Elijah</note> predicted many things by his cabalistic numbers. Likewise, the ancient sages learned to know God equally through this natural and mystical art, and persisted and walked more firmly in His commandments by laws of this kind.

...they declared. Similarly, it is held in the Book of Samuel that the <term>Beelistics</term> <note>original: *Beelisticos*; likely referring to practitioners of ancient or "heathen" divination associated with the name Beelzebub, whom the author argues were not necessarily evil</note> did not follow the fate of the Devil, but instead, by divine permission, were participants in true visions and apparitions. We shall argue this point more extensively in the book *On Supercelestials*.

A gift of this kind was granted by the Lord God to priests walking in divine precepts. It was the custom among the Persians to admit no one as king unless he was a <term>Sophos</term> <note>From the Greek *sophos*, meaning a wise man or philosopher</note>, elevated in both deed and name; this is indeed evident from the very title of their kings, the *Sophi*. Such also were those Persian wise men and <term>Magi</term> <note>Ancient priest-sages of the East</note> who sought Jesus Christ from the East, and who were called "natural priests." 

The Egyptians also, having acquired this magic and philosophy from the Chaldeans and Persians, desired their own priests to learn this same wisdom. --- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the history of the "art" (alchemy and natural philosophy), tracing its lineage from the post-Flood era through the ancient Near East to the Greeks.</meta>

->OF THE PHILOSOPHERS. 11<-

Afterwards, when the son of Noah occupied the third part of the world after the <term>Flood<note>Refers to the biblical Great Flood; in alchemical history, it was believed that antediluvian wisdom had to be rediscovered or preserved through Noah's lineage.</note></term>, this art broke into Chaldea and Persia with a certain force, and from there into Egypt. When the superstitious and idolatrous Greeks got wind of this, some among them who were more sagacious betook themselves to the Chaldeans and the Egyptians, so that they might draw this same wisdom from their schools.

However, since the theological or biblical study of the <term>Mosaic law<note>The laws and teachings attributed to Moses in the Pentateuch; many Renaissance hermeticists believed Moses was a primary source of alchemical knowledge.</note></term> did not appeal to them, they relied instead on their own genius and fell away from the right foundation of such natural secrets and arts. This is evident by example, since they thought so mythically about the doctrine of Moses and faltered.

It was the custom of the Egyptians to propose such traditions of excellent wisdom only through figures, enigmas, and abstruse stories and terms. These were later shadowed forth by <term>Homer<note>The author suggests that Homer’s epics are actually encoded alchemical or philosophical allegories, a common belief in the 16th and 17th centuries.</note></term> with the wondrous artifice of his poems. Then came Pythagoras, who mixed in many things from the law of Moses and the Old Testament. Likewise Hippocrates, Thales of Miletus, Anaxagoras, Democritus, and others did not cease to apply their talents to these things. Although none of them was truly skilled in Astrology, Geometry, Arithmetic, or Medicine. Doubtless their pride hindered them, for it was unwilling to admit the disciples of other nations. And when they had perceived something from the Chaldeans and Egyptians, they became far more proud than

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the nature of religion, specifically focusing on how human characteristics are attributed to the divine.</meta>

42

# It is man who cares for God <note>In this philosophical context, this likely suggests that the human subject is the vessel through which the divine is realized or conceptualized.</note>

This definition of religion appears in two forms:

## 1. Anthropomorphism.
God is conceived under the form of man. By this is meant:
α) That the external form is attributed to him.
β) That the nature of the human spirit is attributed to him.
γ) That the moral failings of man are transferred to God.

The first and second take place in religion, and can nevermore be removed from it. In that respect, namely, that God is present in man, and that we cannot think of God otherwise than as man. Although God is not a man, he is nevertheless to be regarded as man. Because the spirit can only grasp itself under spiritual determinations. The form is the attribute <note>Original: *Beiwort*. In this context, it refers to a defining characteristic or epithet that identifies a person.</note> of the person.

## 2. Anthropopathism. <note>From the Greek *anthropos* (man) and *pathos* (suffering/feeling); the practice of ascribing human emotions or passions to God.</note>
Suffering and human affects are attributed to God. Here, a distinction must be made between what is appropriate and inappropriate.

It is appropriate when we attribute repentance, joy, anger, and jealousy to God. For example: 
* The repentance of God — that is, the nature of the object has changed. <note>A common theological explanation: when the Bible speaks of God "repenting," it describes a change in the human relationship to God or a change in circumstances, rather than a change in God’s own immutable nature.</note>
* The anger of God — that is, the sin of man. 

But it is inappropriate to attribute to him affects of weakness.

<summary>This page explores the concepts of anthropomorphism and anthropopathism in religion, arguing that while attributing human form and spirit to God is necessary for human understanding, a distinction must be made between appropriate and inappropriate emotional attributions.</summary>
<keywords>anthropomorphism, anthropopathism, philosophy of religion, divine nature, human spirit, repentance of God, divine anger</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion regarding the disagreements between various ancient schools of thought concerning the nature of wisdom and philosophy.</meta>

->OF PHILOSOPHERS. 13<-

...as far as it pertains to them, they remained in disagreement. Pythagoras, for this reason, did not wish to be called a <term>Sophus</term> <note>original: "Sophus"; a Greek term for a "Sage" or "Wise Man"</note>, because the Egyptian priesthood and their wisdom were not taught perfectly—as would have been proper—but only minimally. This was despite the fact that he nonetheless brought back many mysteries and secrets from there. Anaxagoras also brought back many things, as is evident from the discussions he held regarding the sun and its stone <note>Anaxagoras famously claimed the sun was not a god, but a red-hot stone or mass of metal</note>, and from the writings he left behind after his death. However, he was opposed to the Egyptians in many matters.

For this reason, they themselves did not wish to be called Sages, nor <term>Magi</term> <note>a term for ancient practitioners of sacred wisdom and natural philosophy</note>; instead, imitating Pythagoras in this regard, they assumed the name of "philosophy" <note>literally "the love of wisdom," implying a pursuit rather than full possession of it</note>. Yet, they themselves brought back nothing but certain sparks, like shadows, from the Magic of the Persians and Egyptians.

In truth, Moses, Abraham, Solomon, Adam, Elijah, and the <term>Magi</term> coming from the East to Christ were the true Magi and divine Sages, as well as <term>Cabalists</term> <note>practitioners of Kabbalah, a form of Jewish mysticism involving the study of divine mysteries</note>. Of this art and wisdom, the Greeks experienced very little or nothing at all. Therefore, we leave behind the "philosophical wisdom" of these Greeks as a mere speculation, distant and separated by a great margin from other true arts and sciences.

### Chapter Four.
### What sort of Magi the Chaldeans, Persians, and Egyptians were.

**M**any have attempted both to scrutinize and to put into practice the most secret magic of these wise men. Nevertheless, this has been done thus far...

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 40, concluding a previous point before transitioning into a comparison between Magic and Kabbalah.</meta>

41          ->AVRORA<-

...did not come to pass. Many in our own time exalt Trithemius <note>Johannes Trithemius (1462–1516), a German abbot and polymath known for his writings on steganography and magic.</note>, others Bacon <note>Likely Roger Bacon, the 13th-century philosopher associated with early experimental science and alchemy.</note>, and Agrippa <note>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa (1486–1535), author of the influential "Three Books of Occult Philosophy."</note>, on account of Magic and Kabbalah. These two disciplines appear to agree very little, and many people are ignorant of why they practice them.

Magic is indeed an art and a faculty through which one reaches the properties, powers, and hidden operations of the elements, bodies, and their fruits. But Kabbalah itself, through <term>anagoge: a method of mystical or spiritual interpretation that rises above the literal meaning</term>, seems to build a path for men toward God: specifically, how one must act with Him and how to prophesy from Him. 

For Kabbalah is full of divine mysteries, just as Magic is full of natural secrets; indeed, the former <note>Kabbalah</note> teaches how to speak of portents based on the nature of things present and future. The operation of Magic, however, consists in knowing all creatures—both celestial and earthly bodies—from the inside out: what is hidden within them, what powers lie dormant, for what purpose they were destined from the beginning, and with what properties they are endowed. 

These and similar things are the bond by which celestial things are joined to earthly ones, so that it can sometimes be perceived even by the eyes and the senses through their operations. Such a conjunction of celestial influences, or the "impression" through which celestial powers act upon lower bodies, was once called <term>Gamahea: a term used by Renaissance magi to describe objects or talismans naturally imbued with stellar influence</term> <note>original: "Gamahea Magis"</note>, or the "marriage" of celestial powers and properties with elementary bodies. From this source flowed the most excellent mixtures of all bodies, both celestial and earth-

<summary>The author distinguishes between Magic, which explores the hidden powers of the natural world, and Kabbalah, which provides a mystical path to the divine, while introducing the concept of "Gamahea" as the marriage of heaven and earth.</summary>
<keywords>Magic, Kabbalah, Trithemius, Agrippa, Roger Bacon, anagoge, Gamahea, celestial influences, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the spiritual and natural light inherent in the world, likely following a section defining the types of divine and earthly knowledge.</meta>

->OF THE PHILOSOPHERS. 15<-

...of earthly things, namely of the Sun and the planets, and likewise of plants, minerals, and animals. The Devil attempted with all his efforts and all his strength to obscure this kind of light. Nor was he entirely frustrated in his hope, since he deprived all of Greece of it, and in its place introduced human speculations and mere blasphemies against God and His Son among those nations. 

Magic truly derived its origin from the divine Ternary <term>Ternary: A group of three, referring here to both the Holy Trinity and a threefold structural pattern found throughout nature.</term> and from the Trinity of God. For God marked all creatures with this Ternary, and with His own finger He engraved this symbolic <note>original: "Hieroglyphicum"; here meaning a sacred or hidden seal of meaning</note> Ternary upon them. Thus, nothing in the nature of things can be identified or brought forth that lacks this mastery of the divine Ternary, or that does not demonstrate it to the eye. 

Indeed, the creature teaches us to understand and see the Creator Himself, as the divine Paul also testifies in his letter to the Romans <note>original: "ad Rom. testatur"; a reference to Romans 1:20, which suggests God's invisible nature is understood through the things He made.</note>. This covenant of the divine Ternary, which is diffused through the entire substance of things, is indissoluble. Through it, we also possess the secrets of all nature derived from the four elements. 

For the Ternary, when combined with the magic Quaternary <term>Quaternary: The number four, representing the material world and the four elements of earth, air, fire, and water.</term>, produces the perfect Septenary <term>Septenary: The number seven, traditionally viewed in alchemy and mysticism as a number of completion or spiritual perfection.</term>, which is endowed with many secrets and demonstrated by visible signs. And when the Quaternary rests within the Ternary, the light of the world rises upon the horizon of eternity, and reveals to us our entire bond with God. To these are also added the powers and operations of all creatures, and their use through their own secrets, signs, and charac— <note>The text breaks off mid-word: "caracte-" likely intended to be "caracteres" (characters or symbols).</note>

<summary>This page argues that magic and natural science are rooted in a "Divine Ternary" (a threefold pattern) established by God within all creation. ...marked with characters and figures, so that in them scarcely the smallest hidden point is left behind which is not revealed by examination. Since where the four and the three ascend to ten <note>original: "quaternarius & ternarius ad denarium." This refers to the Pythagorean concept where 1+2+3+4=10, suggesting that the complexity of the elements (4) and the spiritual principles (3) return to a perfect divine unity (10/1).</note>, there is their return to unity. In that is concluded all the hidden wisdom of things, which God made manifest to men, both through His word and through the creatures of His hands, so that they might have true knowledge of them; a matter which is declared more broadly in another place.

## Chapter five.
### Concerning the most powerful and supreme essence of things.

The **Magi** <note>The "Magi" here refers to the ancient wise men and natural philosophers, often associated with alchemical and Hermetic traditions.</note> affirmed through their wisdom that all creatures are to be brought back to a **Unified substance**. They assert that through its various cleansings and purifications, this substance ascends to such great subtlety, divine nature, and hidden property that it performs wonders. For they considered that it would return to earth and that, through the supreme magical separation, a certain perfect substance would emerge. This substance is at last raised—through many industrious and very lengthy preparations—from vegetable substances into minerals, from minerals into metallic forms, and from perfect metallic substances into a perpetual and divine **fifth essence** <term>Quintessence: In alchemy, this is the 'quinta essentia,' the purest and most concentrated medicinal or spiritual force that remains after the four elements (earth, air, fire, water) have been purified.</term>, which includes within itself the essence of all celestial and earthly creatures...

<summary>This page concludes a reflection on how numerical patterns reveal divine wisdom and begins Chapter Five, which explores the alchemical refinement of matter into the "fifth essence."</summary>
<keywords>Magi, unity, the decad, quintessence, alchemical separation, transmutation, divine wisdom</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the origins of alchemical knowledge, transitioning from the methods of ancient civilizations to the specific terminology used by philosophers to hide their secrets.</meta>

->OF THE PHILOSOPHERS. 17<-

...concluding. The Arabs and Greeks, through hidden characters <note>original: "caracteres occultos"</note> and the hieroglyphic descriptions of the Persians and Egyptians, arrived at secret and abstruse mysteries. Having attained these, and understanding them partly, while partly not, they witnessed certain marvelous things through direct observation. But because super-celestial <note>The term "super-celestial" refers to divine or spiritual operations that exist beyond the physical influence of the stars and planets.</note> operations lay hidden deeper than their grasp could reach, they did not call this secret "super-celestial" according to the opinion of the Magi. Instead, they called it the "secret of the philosophers," and following the opinion of Pythagoras, they called it "their stone" <note>original: "lapidem eorum," referring to the Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold and granting long life.</note>. Whoever attained this stone shrouded it in various enigmatic figures, deceptive similarities, comparisons, and fictional terms; they did this so that its material would remain hidden from future generations, ensuring that no knowledge of it—or at least the very minimum—could be obtained.

### Chapter six.
### Concerning various errors regarding the matter, its discovery, and its understanding.

**P**hilosophers have given various names to this most hidden matter of the stone, drawn from similarities. Observing this in the *Rosary* <note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum* (Rosary of the Philosophers), a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise attributed to Arnaldus de Villa Nova.</note>, Arnaldus <note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311), a celebrated physician and alchemist.</note> says that the greatest difficulty exists in the discovery of the matter of this stone. For they called it vegetable, mineral, and animal, though not according to the literal sense, as those wise in divine secrets well know.

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the common misconceptions of alchemical materials, specifically addressing the metaphorical use of plant names by earlier masters like Raymond Lully.</meta>

16 ->AVRORA<-

...and have experienced the miracles of such a stone. An example of this is shown in the **Moonwort** <note>original: "lunaria"; a plant also known as "Honesty," which was often used by alchemists as a symbol for the silver-making process due to its moon-like seed pods</note> of **Raymond Lully** <note>A 13th-century Franciscan philosopher to whom many influential alchemical works were later attributed</note>, which produces flowers of wondrous virtues well known to the philosophers themselves. Yet it was not their intention that this plant should be understood as a means for <term>projection</term> <note>The final stage of alchemy, where a small amount of the "Stone" is cast upon molten base metal to transmute it into gold or silver</note> onto metals, or that such a preparation should be made from it; instead, the hidden mind of the philosophers looks toward something else. 

In the same way, they called their primary matter by the name **Martagon** <note>The Turk's-cap lily; here used as a cryptic code-name for a specific mineral or chemical state</note>, to which they applied a hidden alchemical operation, even though that name signifies nothing other than a very secret resemblance. 

A significant error also arose regarding the juices of plants, with which many have attempted to <term>coagulate</term> <note>To solidify a liquid; in this context, turning liquid mercury into a solid metal</note> mercury, and afterwards to convert it into silver <note>original: "lunam"</note> using "fixing waters." They believed that whoever could achieve solidification in this way, without the help of other metals, had attained the "Great Mastery" <note>The *magisterium*, or the successful completion of the alchemical work</note>. 

And although the juices of some plants do accomplish this solidification, it is judged to happen for no other reason than the resin, oiliness, or earthy sulfur in which they abound. This draws the moisture and "life" of the mercury to itself, mixing with its substance and solidifying it to no useful purpose. For I have discovered that no "thick" or external sulfur found in plants is suitable for perfect projection in alchemy—a fact that many have learned through experience, and at a very great financial loss.

<summary>The author warns against taking alchemical plant names like 'Lunaria' or 'Martagon' literally, explaining that using plant juices to solidify mercury is a costly mistake that produces no true silver.</summary>

### Chapter seven.
### On the errors of those who seek the stone from vegetables, as mentioned above.

Some Alchemists boiled down the juice expressed from celandine to a thickness or consistency, and exposed it to the sun so that it might coagulate into a hard mass by itself. This mass, afterwards crushed into a very fine powder of a black or reddish-brown color, was supposed to turn mercury into gold <note>original: "solem", literally "the sun," the alchemical symbol for gold</note> by means of projection <note>The act of casting a small amount of a transmuting powder onto a molten base metal</note>; however, they found this to be in vain. Others mixed sal ammoniac <note>term: sal armoniacum; ammonium chloride, used to help minerals react or dissolve</note> into this powder; others used *colcothar* of vitriol <note>iron(III) oxide, a red residue left after the distillation of sulfuric acid</note>, believing that by this way they could reach their desired goal. They brought these into solutions as a yellow water, so that the sal ammoniac might provide the tincture with an entry into the substance of mercury...

### Chapter Eight

*Concerning those who have sought the Philosophers’ Stone in animal substances.*

**B**y a certain figurative name, they said their matter was "virgin’s milk" <note>original: "lac virginis"</note> and the "blessed blood of a rosy color," though these terms are actually suited only for prophets and the sons of God. From this, the Sophists <note>In this context, "Sophists" refers to false alchemists or those who misinterpret the allegorical language of the true masters.</note> concluded that this philosophical matter exists in animal blood, or even in human blood, seizing on the logic that animals are nourished by vegetable matter. Others sought it in hair, in the salt of urine, and in Rebis <note>original: "Rebis." While this often refers to the alchemical hermaphrodite, here it likely refers to human excrement or a composite waste product.</note>. Others looked in hen's eggs, in milk, and in the lime made from their shells: by all of which they thought they could fix mercury <term>fixation: the chemical or alchemical process of turning a volatile, liquid, or gaseous substance (like mercury) into a solid, stable form that can endure fire.</term>.

Some extracted salt from foul-smelling wash <note>This refers to aged or "putrefied" urine.</note>, believing it to be the matter of the stone. There were even those who judged that small stones found in the Rebis <note>Likely referring to kidney or bladder stones.</note> were the true matter. Others macerated the membranes of eggs in the harshest lye, to which they also mixed the calcined shells of eggs, white as snow, attributing to these the secret of fixation for transforming mercury. Others, comparing the white of eggs to silver and the yolk to gold, chose them for their matter, adding common salt, sal ammoniac <note>A mineral salt (ammonium chloride) used as a flux in metallurgy.</note>, and tartar;

### Chapter Nine.

->Concerning those who sought the stone in animals<-

The ignorant have approached, believing the stone to be threefold, and divided into three distinct kinds: namely, vegetable, animal, and mineral. Because of this, they sought it among minerals. This opinion is far removed from the views of the philosophers. For they [the true philosophers] assert that their stone is uniformly vegetable, animal, and mineral <note>the author argues the Stone is a unity of all three kingdoms of nature, not three separate things</note>. It should be noted here that nature has distributed its mineral "seed" <note>original: *sperma*</note> into various categories: namely, into sulfurs, salts, boraxes, nitres, <term>ammoniacs</term> <note>sal ammoniac</note>, alums, arsenics, <term>atraments</term> <note>vitriolic substances used for making ink</note>, vitriols, <term>tutias</term> <note>zinc oxide</note>, hematites, <term>orpiments</term> <note>yellow arsenic sulfide</note>, <term>realgars</term> <note>red arsenic sulfide</note>, magnesias, cinnabar, antimony, talc, <term>cachymia</term> <note>an imperfect metallic ore or dross</note>, marcasites, and so on. In all of these, nature has not yet fully secured our [true] matter. Although in some of the mentioned types, it reveals itself in a marvelous appearance regarding the transmutation of imperfect metals that are to be brought to perfection. For long experience and the practice of fire demonstrate a manifold transformation in the matter of minerals; and not only from colors into [other] colors,

...colors, but also from one essence into another, and from its own imperfection into perfection. Although nature also, through mineral means, has pursued some degree of perfection by various preparations, the philosophers do not, for that reason, intend for the matter of their **Philosopher’s Stone** to proceed from any of the common minerals, even though they may call their stone "mineral" in nature. 

Hence the <term>Sophists</term> <note>In alchemical literature, 'Sophists' refers to those who follow the letter of recipes without understanding the true natural philosophy, often resulting in failed or dangerous experiments.</note>, taking this as their pretext, have persecuted mercury itself with various tortures: some by sublimations, coagulations, precipitations, mercurial waters, <term>strong waters</term> <note>original: "aquis fortibus"; refers to nitric acid or other powerful mineral acids</note>, and so on. All these erroneous ways must be avoided, along with the other mineral preparations of the Sophists, such as their purgings and the <term>fixations</term> <note>The process of making a volatile substance stable under heat.</note> of spirits and metals. 

Therefore, all preparations of the Stone—such as those described by **Geber**, **Albertus Magnus**, and similar authors—are sophisticated <note>meaning false or deceptive in this context</note>. Their purgings, cementations, sublimations, distillations, rectifications, circulations, putrefactions, conjunctions, solutions, ascensions, coagulations, calcinations, and <term>incerations</term> <note>The process of softening a substance to a wax-like consistency.</note> are utterly useless. This applies whether they are performed in the tripod, the <term>athanor</term> <note>A furnace designed to maintain a uniform heat over a long period.</note>, the reverberatory furnace, the melting furnace, the <unclear>accidioneo</unclear> <note>Likely a specific type of heating vessel or furnace, possibly related to "acedineum" or an assaying tool.</note>, or using dung, ash, sand, and the like. It applies equally to their use of the cucurbit, the <term>pelican</term> <note>A specialized circulatory vessel with two side-arms that feed back into the main body.</note>, the retort, the vial, the fixatory, and so forth. 

The same judgment must be held regarding the sublimation of mercury through mineral spirits toward the **white** and the **red** <note>These refer to the *albedo* and *rubedo*, the final stages of the Great Work which produce the white stone (transmuting silver) and the red stone (transmuting gold).</note>, such as through vitriol, saltpeter, alum, or <term>crocus Martis</term> <note>A reddish-brown powder, usually iron oxide.</note>. Concerning all of which,

That <term>Sophist</term> <note>In alchemical texts, a 'Sophist' refers to a false alchemist or someone who uses deceptive logic rather than true natural philosophy.</note>, Johannes de Rupescissa, falsely tells tales in his treatise on the **philosopher’s stone**, both the white and the red. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**Chapter Ten.**

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*Concerning those who sought the stone and the particulars*

Others purified <term>vitriol</term> <note>A sulfate mineral, often iron or copper sulfate</note> seven times through calcination, solution, and coagulation, and with the addition of two parts of <term>sal ammoniac</term> <note>original: "armoniaci salis," ammonium chloride, used to make acids more potent</note> and sublimation, so that they might resolve it into a white water—

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the failed methods used by "Sophists" (false alchemists) to prepare the materials for the Philosopher's Stone. The previous page likely described initial steps involving various salts or minerals.</meta>

->[OF THE] PHILOSOPHERS. 27<-

...[continued], to which they added a third part of **quicksilver** <note>original: *mercurij viui*; liquid mercury</note>, so that the water might be coagulated. They afterwards sublimated that mercury so many times with the aforementioned **vitriol** <note>a sulfate mineral</note> and **sal ammoniac** <note>original: *armo-niaco fale*; ammonium chloride</note> until it turned into a stone. They claimed that this substance, conceived from the vitriol, was the **"Red Sulfur of the Philosophers,"** with which they proceeded through various solutions and coagulations toward the [Philosopher's] Stone; but in the final projection <note>the act of casting the alchemical powder onto molten metal to transform it</note>, they achieved nothing.

Others coagulated mercury using alum water into a hard mass resembling alum, which they then attempted to "fix" <note>to make a volatile substance stable under heat</note> with "fixing waters," but to no avail. The **Sophists** <note>a term used for alchemists who used deceptive or purely chemical methods rather than the "true" philosophical path</note> set before themselves many ways of fixing mercury in vain, because within mercury, there is nothing permanent or perfect to be found. 

Thus, minerals are added to it in vain through these deceptive processes; by all these means, the mercury is only stirred to a greater "malice" <note>metaphorical term for the metal's volatile and unmanageable nature</note>, becoming even more "alive" <note>fluid and volatile</note> and falling into greater impurity rather than reaching any state of perfection. Therefore, the true material of the Philosophers should not be sought from it. It is an imperfect thing, and even if it could be brought to perfection, the methods of the Sophists would find it extremely difficult—indeed, impossible—since there is nothing in common mercury that can be stirred or forced into perfection. 

Some took **arsenic** that had been sublimated <note>purified by turning it into vapor and then solidifying it again</note> several times, and dissolved it repeatedly in **oil of tartar** <note>a concentrated solution of potassium carbonate</note>, then coagulated it. They claimed this would "fix" the substance so they could turn copper into silver. However, this yielded nothing except a certain "deceptive whitening" <note>original: *sophisticam dealbationem*; a surface-level change in color that mimics silver without changing the metal's nature</note>.

28                                  AURORA

...bore itself. For indeed, arsenic certainly cannot be **fixed** <note>In alchemy, "fixing" a substance means to make a volatile or gaseous material stable so that it can withstand heat without evaporating.</note> at all unless the skilled practitioner has a thorough knowledge of its **staining spirit** <note>original: *spiritū eius tingentem*</note>, a matter in which all philosophers have slumbered, striving in vain to produce anything from it.

Whoever, therefore, is ignorant of this spirit should have no hope of fixing the arsenic itself, nor of granting it the power by which it might achieve the perfection of **transmutation**. For this reason, I make it known to all that the **whitening** <note>Latin: *dealbationem*</note> previously mentioned is drawn from a false foundation; by it, copper is falsely whitened but not truly changed. 

**Sophists** <note>In alchemical texts, "sophists" refers to fraudulent practitioners or "puffers" who used deceptive chemical tricks rather than true alchemy.</note> have taken **Venus** <note>The alchemical name for copper.</note> masked and disguised in this manner, mixed it with twice its weight of the **Moon** <note>The alchemical name for silver.</note>, and sold it to goldsmiths and mint-masters. Ultimately, they turned themselves into counterfeiters—not only those who sold it, but even those who bought it. 

Some of these sophists took red arsenic instead of white, and this itself resulted in a false art. For in whatever way it is prepared, it shows nothing but whiteness. Others went further to common sulfur; they cooked the yellow substance in vinegar, lye, or very sharp urine, day and night, until it emerged white. Afterward, they "raised" it from common salt and **lime of eggs** <note>Powdered, calcined eggshells, used as a purifying agent.</note>; they cooked and **sublimated** <note>The process of heating a substance into a vapor so it collects as a solid in the upper part of a vessel.</note> it many times. Nevertheless, it always remained combustible, even though it was white; yet they tried to use it to fix raw **mercu-** <note>The text cuts off mid-word; likely *mercurium* (mercury).</note>

Others reduced common sulfur into the form of a "liver" <note>original: *hæpatis*; referring to "liver of sulfur," a mixture of potassium sulfides named for its dark color</note> by mixing in linseed oil, oil of bricks, or olive oil, and boiling it in vinegar. Just as the number of minerals is infinite, so too is there a great variety of their preparations. I have deferred making a broader mention of these in this place because they require a specialized treatise. 

One must also beware of "sophistic" <note>original: *sophisticis*; meaning deceptive, fraudulent, or chemically altered to look like something it is not</note> oils of vitriol <note>sulfuric acid</note> and antimony. Likewise, one should avoid the oils of both imperfect and perfect metals, whether of gold or silver. For although the operation of these is most powerful in the nature of things, the true process, up to this very day, is known to very few. One must also abstain from the deceptive preparations of common mercury, arsenic, sulfur, and other similar things—namely those made by sublimation, by descent, and by fixation with vinegar, saltpeter, tartar, vitriol, and sal ammoniac <note>ammonium chloride</note>.

From these, I will teach the true projection <note>the act of casting the alchemical agent upon a base metal to transform it</note> upon mercury and imperfect metals.

### Chapter Eleven.
**Concerning the true and perfect particular secret from arsenic for the white tincture.** <note>The "white tincture" is the stage of the Great Work that transmutes base metals into silver.</note>

Some have written that arsenic is composed of mercury and sulfur; others of earth and water; and most from the nature of sulfur. In whatever way it may be, its nature is such that it transmutes red copper into white. It can even be brought to such perfection of preparation that it tints truly and perfectly. This is not done by the way taught by the wicked sophists, such as Geber <note>original: "Geber in summa perfectionis"; referring to a famous medieval alchemical text attributed to Jabir ibn Hayyan</note> in the *Sum of Perfection*, or Albertus Magnus,

...[Ma]gnus, Aristotle the Chemist in his book *Of the Perfect Magistery*, <note>original: "libro perfecti magisterij"</note> Rasis, and Polydorus: for all these writers, as many as there are, err—either out of envy they write falsehoods and propose common recipes, or because they were simply ignorant of the truth. Arsenic contains within itself three natural spirits:

First, a volatile, combustible, and corrosive spirit that penetrates all metals. This spirit whitens <term>Venus|The alchemical name for Copper</term>, and after several days renders it spongy. This craftsmanship belongs only to those who practice the caustic arts. 

The second spirit is crystalline and sweet. 

The third is a <term>tingeing spirit|A "tincture" or spirit capable of permanently changing the color and nature of a metal</term>, separated from the others previously mentioned. The true philosophers seek these three natural properties in arsenic for the perfect <term>projection|The final stage of the Great Work, where the alchemical catalyst is "projected" onto base metals to transmute them</term> of the wise.

However, the <term>barber-surgeons|original: "Barbitofores," referring to those who performed both hair-cutting and minor surgery</term> who practice surgery experience the sweet and crystalline nature—separated from the tingeing spirit—for use in healing wounds, and for <term>buboes|Inflammatory swellings of the lymph nodes</term>, carbuncles, <term>anthrax boils|original: "antraces"; refers here to burning, malignant skin ulcers</term>, and other such defects of ulcers, which must be cured by a mild artifice. 

Furthermore, that tingeing spirit—unless its pure part is separated from the impure, the fixed from the volatile, and the secret tincture from the combustible—will by no means achieve the <term>projection</term> upon mercury, Venus, or any other imperfect metal according to one's desire. All philosophers have hidden this secret as a most excellent mystery. Such a tingeing spirit...

### Chapter Twelve.

### Concerning the secret of Vitriol, and the red tincture to be extracted from it.

**V**itriol <note>a sulfate mineral, usually iron or copper sulfate, prized for its corrosive and transformative properties</note>, a very noble mineral among others, has always been held in the highest esteem by the **philosophers** <note>the alchemists</note>, because God the Most High has adorned this substance with wonderful gifts above all others. They have veiled its secret with figures and riddles, such as this: **Visit the interior of the earth, and by rectifying you will find the hidden stone, the true medicine.** <note>original: "Visitabis interiora terrae, rectificando invenies occultum lapidem veram medicinam." The first letters of these Latin words form the acronym V.I.T.R.I.O.L.</note> By "earth" they understood vitriol itself, and by "the interior of the earth," they meant its sweetness and its redness. For in the hidden part of subtle vitriol lies a noble and most fragrant juice, and a pure oil.

<meta>The previous page appears to have been discussing the hidden nature of substances and the preparation of philosophical secrets. This page continues with instructions on how to extract the 'arcanum' or secret essence from plants and minerals without destroying their vital force.</meta>

->OF THE PHILOSOPHERS. 33<-

...is hidden. The method of its extraction <note>original: prolectionis</note> must by no means be attempted by calcination <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures, often to the point of reduction to ash, to remove volatile impurities</term> or distillation. For indeed, it must not be deprived of its greenness <note>viriditas: in alchemy, this refers to the vital "green" life force or energy within a substance</note>; for as soon as it is stripped of this, it must necessarily lack its secret essence <term>arcanum: the hidden healing virtue or the "star" of a substance that contains its true medicinal power</term> and its powers. It should be noted by both <note>likely referring to the two categories of minerals and plants mentioned next</note> in this place, that not only minerals, but even the plants themselves, and similar things showing greenness on the outside, contain within them a red oil like blood, which is their secret essence. 

From this it is clear that the distillations of the pharmacists <note>original: pharmacopeorum</note> are useless, vain, and foolish, and of no importance, because they do not know how to draw out the blood-red color of the plants. Wise nature herself turns the waters of all plants into a lemon-yellow color, and after that, into an oil as red as blood. The primary reason this transformation happens more slowly is the excessive haste of ignorant distillation workers, by which the greenness is consumed. They have not learned to strengthen nature with its own powers, through which that noble greenness ought to be rectified <term>rectification: the process of refining a substance through repeated distillation or maturation to reach its purest state</term> into redness by itself. 

White wine is an example, maturing <note>original: digerens</note> itself into a lemon-yellow color; and over time, the green color of its grape cluster <note>original: botri</note> turns by itself into a red that was hidden under the blue. Therefore, when the greenness of plants and minerals is lost through the laziness of the workers, the essence as well as the spirit of the oil and the balsam of the most noble secrets <note>original: arcanorum</note> will have perished.

### Chapter Thirteen.
->On the process of Vitriol for the red tincture.<-

**Vitriol** <term>Vitriol: A metallic sulfate, in this case likely "green vitriol" or iron(II) sulfate, which was a cornerstone of alchemical practice.</term> contains within itself many muddy and viscous <note>original: lutosas & viscosas</note> imperfections: for which reason its watery greenness must be drawn out quite often and rectified, until it sheds all the impurities of the earth. Once all these rectifications are finished, one must be especially careful that the matter is not exposed to the sun, because the sun’s power turns its greenness into paleness, and the **arcanum** <term>arcanum: the secret, "inner" power or medicinal essence of a substance.</term> is simultaneously absorbed. It should be kept covered in a warm **hypocaust** <term>hypocaust: originally a Roman underfloor heating system, but here referring to a heated chamber or stove providing gentle, steady warmth.</term>, so that it is not fouled by dust.

Afterwards, let it be digested in a sealed glass for the space of several months, and for so long until various colors appear, and finally a supreme redness. Yet the redness should not be considered sufficiently "fixed" <note>In alchemy, a "fixed" substance is one that can withstand heat without evaporating or changing.</note> by this process alone; rather, it must be further purified from internal and accidental impurities of the earth in this manner: It must be rectified with vinegar until the earthy impurity is entirely removed and the dregs are taken away. This is the true and best rectification of its tincture, from which the **blessed oil** <note>original: benedictum oleum; a highly purified liquid extract believed to have miraculous healing or transformative properties.</note> is to be drawn.

From this, having sealed the glass more diligently with an **alembic** <term>alembic: the "cap" or "head" of a distilling apparatus that catches the rising vapors.</term> placed on top and the joints sealed with bitumen <note>a process called "luting," where joints were sealed with clay or resin to prevent the escape of vapors.</note> so that the spirit does not exhale, the spirit of this oil is to be drawn forth with a gentle and slow fire. This oil is more delightful and sweeter than any apothecary's balsam or aromatic, lacking all sharpness. There will remain in the bottom of the **cucurbit** <term>cucurbit: the gourd-shaped lower vessel of a still containing the liquid to be distilled.</term> a certain very white earth, shining and glowing.

->OF THE PHILOSOPHERS. 35<-

...which is like snow; preserve and protect this from all dust. This very earth has been entirely separated from its redness. Then follows the greatest secret, namely the super-celestial marriage of the soul—highly purified and washed through the blood of the lamb <note>original: "agni sanguinem." This is a Christological metaphor used in alchemy to describe a potent, purifying red solvent or the "tincture" that cleanses the base matter.</note>—with its shining, bright, and purified body. This is the true super-celestial marriage, which is prolonged until the final, destined day.

In this manner, therefore, the soul and the spirit of <term>vitriol|A historical term for sulfate minerals or sulfuric acid, considered a primary material in the search for the Philosopher's Stone</term> (which exist as its blood) are joined with their purified body, so that they may be inseparable for eternity. Keep such a union, placed in the furnace of secrets, for forty days. When these have passed, you will have a most absolute oil of wonderful perfection, by which mercury and any other imperfect metals are turned into gold.

Now, let us apply our minds to <term>multiplication|The process of increasing the quantity and the transmuting power of the alchemical medicine</term>. Take "corporeal" mercury in a portion of two parts, which you should soak with three parts by weight of the aforementioned oil, and let them remain together for forty days. By this proportion of weight and order, multiplication is achieved unto infinity.

**Chapter Fourteen,**
*Concerning the secrets and mysteries of antimony, for the red tincture, for transmutation.*

**A**ntimony is the true bath of gold. <note>In alchemy, the "bath of gold" refers to the process where antimony was used to purify gold; the antimony would "consume" other metals mixed with the gold, leaving the gold pure.</note> Philosophers, however, have called it the "Examiner" and <term>Stilangis</term> <note>original: "Stilangem." Likely a specialized alchemical term for a purifying agent or a specific vessel.</note>. Poets truly say that in that bath, Vulcan <note>The Roman god of fire, representing the alchemical furnace.</note> washed Phoebus <note>The sun god, representing gold.</note>, and cleansed him from all filth and imperfections. It is born from the purest and most noble mercury and sulfur, of the nature of <term>vitriol</term> <note>A category of metallic sulfates, often associated with green or blue crystalline minerals.</note>, in metallic form and splendor. Some philosophers have called it the "white lead of the wise," or simply "lead." 

Take, therefore, as much as you wish of the very best antimony; let this be dissolved in its own <term>aqua fortis</term> <note>original: "aqua forti"; literally "strong water," referring to nitric acid.</note>, and then cast into cold water. Add a little <term>Saffron of Mars</term> <note>original: "croco Martio"; a chemical term for iron oxide, used here as a catalyst or coloring agent.</note> so that it falls as a sediment to the bottom of the vessel; for otherwise, it does not shed its dregs. After it has been dissolved in this manner, it will have acquired the highest beauty. 

Let it be placed in a glass vessel, fortified all around with the strongest <term>lute</term> <note>A paste or cement made of clay and other materials used to seal vessels and protect them from high heat.</note>, or in a stone jar, and let there be mixed in calcined <term>tutia</term> <note>Zinc oxide, typically collected as a powder from the flues of smelting furnaces.</note>, sublimated to the perfect degree of fire. One must take very accurate care against liquefaction <note>The melting of the solid into a liquid state.</note>, because it bursts the glass with too much heat. From one pound of this antimony, a sublimation is obtained, perfected in the space of two days. This sublimated substance, when placed in a vial so that it reaches a third part of the water— <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence at the bottom of the page.</note>

[The substance] should be placed [within the vessel], with the vessel sealed with clay <term>lutato vase: a technique of sealing laboratory glassware with a paste of clay, lime, or horse dung to prevent the escape of volatile vapors</term> so that the spirit does not escape. While the vapor rises, various colors appear; let the fire be moderated until the red matter is seen.

This is the highest secret of the philosophers regarding antimony, which they value most highly among the secrets of oils.

Take as much of the purest gold as you wish; take care to dissolve this in rectified spirit of wine <term>spiritu vini rectificato: highly concentrated alcohol, often used as a solvent in early chemistry</term>. Let the spirit be drawn off several times, and as many times let it be dissolved again. Let the final solution be kept with the spirit of wine and circulated <term>circulated: a process of continuous distillation and condensation within a sealed vessel, intended to "refine" the substance over time</term> for a month. Afterwards, let the volatile gold and the spirit of wine be distilled through an alembic <note>a pear-shaped distilling apparatus</note> three or four times, so that it flows into the receiving vessel and is brought to its highest essence. To the me—

## Chapter Fifteen.

### On the projection to be made through the mystery and secret of antimony.

No "just weight" <note>meaning a fixed or universal ratio</note> can be assigned in this work of <term>projection</term> <note>the act of casting the alchemical medicine onto base metals to transmute them into gold</note>, even though the <term>tincture</term> <note>the concentrated medicinal essence</note> itself is drawn from a subject in a certain proportion and with the proper instruments. For indeed, this medicine sometimes tints thirty, forty, and sometimes sixty, eighty, or even a hundred parts of imperfect metal. Thus, the "hinge" <note>the most critical point</note> of this business turns most especially upon the cleansing of the medicine and the skill of the operator, as well as according to the greater purity of one metal than another... <note>The text cuts off here at the syllable 'mun-', likely part of 'munditiam' or 'mundationem,' referring to the cleanliness or purity of the metals being transformed.</note>

<summary>This page describes the final unification of dissolved gold and oil of antimony to create a powerful "arcanum" capable of transmuting base metals into gold. It also begins a new chapter on "projection," explaining that the potency of the medicine depends on its purity and the skill of the alchemist.</summary>

<meta>The text begins mid-word with "rius," likely the conclusion of a word like "deterius" (worse) or "proprius" (particular) from the preceding page. This page continues a discussion on the practicalities of alchemical "projection"—the final stage of transmutation.</meta>

### [OF THE] PHILOSOPHERS. 39

...the cleanliness and purity of the imperfect body <note>A base metal, such as copper or lead, intended for transformation</note> taken in hand. Since one Venus <note>original: "venus"; the alchemical name for Copper</note> is purer than another, it follows that a fixed weight cannot always be maintained during projection <note>The final stage of alchemy where the "Stone" or tincture is cast upon base metal to transmute it</note>. 

This alone is worthy of note: if the operator happens to have used too much of the tincture, he can correct this error by adding more imperfect metal. If, however, there is too much of the subject metal, so that the strength of the tincture is weakened, this fault is easily remedied by cupellation <note>original: "cineritio"; a refining process using a shallow porous cup (cupel) made of bone ash</note>, or by cementations, or by washing with crude antimony. 

There is nothing in this part to delay or hinder the worker; let him only keep in mind what has been omitted by the philosophers, and studiously veiled by some: namely, that in projections, the **revivification** of imperfect bodies is necessary—that is, **animation**, or, so to speak, **spiritualization**. Regarding this, several have said that their metals are not the common ones, because they live and possess a soul.

*Animation is performed in this manner.*

Take as much as you wish of Venus <note>Copper</note> beaten into thin plates—ten, twenty, or forty pounds—and let them be encrusted with pastes made from arsenic and calcined tartar <note>Potassium bitartrate, a byproduct of winemaking used here as a chemical reagent</note>. Let them be calcined in their own vessel for the space of twenty-four hours. Then, finally, let the crushed Venus be washed and cleaned most thoroughly. Let the calcination with washing be repeated three or four times...

<summary>This page discusses how to adjust the ratio of "tincture" to metal during transmutation and introduces "animation," a process of preparing copper using arsenic and tartar to give it the "life" necessary for alchemy.</summary>
<keywords>Venus, copper, projection, tincture, cupellation, animation, arsenic, calcined tartar, revivification, spiritualization</keywords>

...by repetitions of fire. In this way, it is cleansed and purged of its thick greenness <note>original: "viriore"; likely referring to the green oxidation or "verdigris" associated with unrefined copper</note> and its impure sulfur. One must certainly beware of calcinations made with common sulfur; for that which is good in the metal, it utterly corrupts, and makes the bad even worse. From this, the most perfect gold arises.

From this, the philosophers concluded that a third thing is necessary: namely, the animated seed of both the male and the female. Without this, they judged their entire work to be void and empty.

## Chapter seventeen.
## Concerning the preparation of the matter of the philosopher's stone.

Nature requires this above all: namely, that its **philosophical man** be brought into a mercurial substance, so that it may be born as the **philosophical stone**. Furthermore, it must be noted that those common preparations of **Geber**, **Albertus Magnus**, **Thomas Aquinas**, **Rupescissa**, **Polydorus**, and those like them <note>These were famous medieval authorities on alchemy and theology.</note>, are nothing other than certain particular solutions, sublimations, and calcinations; they pertain very little to our universal work, which requires only the most secret fire of the philosophers. Therefore, let fire and <term>Azoth</term> <note>Azoth is the 'universal solvent' or the essential spirit of mercury that acts as a transformative agent.</note> be enough for you. Although the philosophers make mention of some preparations,

...you must understand that regarding putrefaction, distillation, sublimation, calcination, coagulation, whitening, reddening, waxing <term>ceration: the process of softening a hard substance to a wax-like consistency</term>, fixation, and so on: in their universal sense, Nature herself fulfills whatever operations are required in the stated matter. It is not the worker who does this, provided the matter is in the philosophical vessel <note>a sealed glass container, often called the "Egg of the Philosophers"</note> and over a "similar" fire, not a common one <note>alchemists distinguished between "common fire," like a kitchen hearth, and a "secret fire" or controlled heat source</note>.

The **White** and the **Red** <note>the *albedo* and *rubedo*, the final two major stages of the alchemical work</note> spring forth from a single root, without any other medium. It is dissolved by itself, and coupled by itself; it becomes white and red, yellow and black, all by its own power. It weds itself, and conceives within itself. To be cooked, roasted, and melted, it both ascends and descends. Indeed, all these operations are but one single operation, performed by fire alone.

Nevertheless, some philosophers have dissolved the body of the **Sun** <note>original: *solis*; refers to Gold</note> through a highly graduated **essence of wine** <term>spirit of wine: a highly distilled alcohol or ethanol used as a powerful solvent</term>, and rendered it volatile so that it might rise through an alembic <note>a distillation apparatus</note>. They thought this was the true "volatile matter" of the philosophers, but it is not. 

And while it is no small secret to bring this perfected metallic body into a volatile and spiritual substance, they nonetheless err in the separation of the elements. For the process used by the monks—namely **Lully** <note>Ramon Llull, a Majorcan mystic; though many alchemical works were falsely attributed to him</note>, **Richard the Englishman**, **Rupescissa** <note>John of Rupescissa, a 14th-century Franciscan who pioneered the use of alcohol in alchemy</note>, and others—is erroneous. By this path, they thought they could separate gold into a subtle, spiritual, and elemental power, each one separately, and then later through circulation...

<meta>The text on this page continues a discussion from page 46 regarding the attempts of alchemists to join and rectify substances, concluding that human intervention often fails where nature succeeds.</meta>

->PHILOSOPHERS. 47<-

...[junc]tion and rectification again into one, but in vain. Indeed, even if one element is somehow separated from another, nevertheless any element separated in this manner can be separated again into another element. These parts afterwards, by means of "pelican-like" circulation <note>The "pelican" was a specific alchemical vessel with two side-arms that allowed condensed vapors to flow back into the main body, creating a constant cycle of purification.</note> or distillation, can by no means return to one again, but they always remain as a certain volatile matter and "potable gold" <note>original: "aurum potabile"; a legendary medicinal liquid gold believed to cure all ailments and grant longevity.</note>, as they call it. 

The reason why they could not reach their goal is this: because nature does not want to be distracted or separated in this way by human divisions, such as through earthly glass vessels and instruments. Nature alone knows her own operations and the "weights" <note>In alchemy, "weight" often refers to the specific proportions of the four elements (Earth, Air, Fire, Water) required for a substance to be balanced.</note> of the elements, whose separations, rectifications, and unions she carries out without the aid of any operator or manual craft, provided the matter is kept in a "secret fire" and in its own hidden vessel. 

Therefore, the separation of elements by human hands is impossible; though it may appear to happen, it is nonetheless not true, regardless of what is said about Raymond Lull <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316) was a famous philosopher; many alchemical texts were falsely attributed to him in later centuries to give them authority.</note> and his "English noble" <note>A gold coin. Legend claimed Lull used alchemy to create the gold for King Edward III to mint these coins.</note> gold coin, which he is falsely thought to have manufactured. For nature herself possesses within herself her own separator, who joins again what he separates without human help. She knows best of all the proportion of each element—and man does not, despite what erroneous writers [may say]...

In this manner, therefore, the names of the philosophical operations are to be understood, and not otherwise.

...not without danger to the body and life of the operator. Conversely, if the vessel is more capacious than allows the heat to work upon the matter in its proper proportion, the work will be useless and frustrated. For this reason, our **philosophical vessel** <note>The *vas philosophicum* is the specialized glass container, often an alembic or retort, designed to circulate vapors without letting them escape.</note> must be fashioned with the utmost diligence.

But what the material of this vessel of ours might be is understood only by those who, in the first **solution** <note>original: *solutione*; the process of dissolving or breaking down a solid substance into its essential liquid form</note> of our fixed and perfect matter, have led it back into its first essence—of which enough has been said. The operator should also most accurately note exactly what the matter releases and casts off from itself during that first solution.

The method for describing the shape of the vessel is difficult: it must be exactly as nature itself requires, sought and investigated from various sources, so that from the height of the **philosophical heaven** <note>The upper portion of the alchemical flask where vapors rise and condense.</note>, raised above the **philosophical earth** <note>The solid sediment or "dead head" remaining at the bottom of the vessel.</note>, it can work upon the fruit of its earthly body. It must certainly possess this form so that the separation and purification of the elements can occur when the fire drives one from another, and so that each element may occupy the place where it belongs. In this way, the **sun** <note>Representing gold or the vital masculine principle in the work.</note> and the other planets may exercise their operations around the elemental earth, and their circular courses are neither hindered nor driven by too rapid a motion. In accordance with everything that has been said, the vessel must have the correct proportion of roundness and height.

Indeed, the instruments for the first **purification** <note>original: *mundificationem*; literally "cleansing" or "world-making" in a metallurgical context.</note> of mineral bodies are melting vessels <note>Crucibles.</note>, bellows, tongs, **cupels** <insert>capel-</insert> <note>The text breaks off at *capel-*, referring to *capellae*, shallow porous cups used in the process of cupellation to refine precious metals.</note>

<summary>This page outlines the vital importance of the "philosophical vessel's" proportions, explaining that the flask must act as a miniature cosmos where heat and matter interact to separate and purify the elements.</summary>
<keywords>philosophical vessel, alchemy, solution, purification, philosophical heaven, philosophical earth, crucibles, cupellation, planetary influence</keywords>

### Chapter Nineteen.
### Concerning the secret fire of the philosophers.

**C**elebrated is this maxim of the philosophers: Fire and <term>Azoth</term> <note>original: "Azoc"; the universal solvent or essential mercurial principle sought by alchemists</note> are sufficient for you; for fire alone is the entire work and the whole art. Furthermore, as many as build their fire with coals are in error, keeping the vessel in that heat. Some have tried in vain with the heat of horse dung. With a fire of coals, without a medium, they <term>sublimated</term> <note>the process of heating a substance into vapor and then collecting it as a solid again</note> the matter, but did not dissolve it. Others produced heat with their lamps, asserting that this was the secret fire of the philosophers for fabricating their stone. Some placed it in a bath, first in heaps of ant eggs; others in juniper ashes; some sought the fire in <term>quicklime</term> <note>calcium oxide, which generates heat when wet</note>, in tartar, vitriol, saltpeter, etc. Others in <term>burning water</term> <note>original: "aqua ardente"; typically refers to distilled alcohol or ethanol</note>, as Thomas Aquinas <note>Referring to the "Pseudo-Aquinas," a collection of alchemical texts falsely attributed to the famous theologian Saint Thomas Aquinas</note> falsely speaks of this fire, saying that God and his angels cannot be without this fire, but use it daily. What blasphemy is this? Is it not a most manifest lie that God cannot be without the elemental fire of "burning water"? All heats produced by fire through the means mentioned are utterly useless for our work. See to it that...

52	AURORA

Do not be led astray by *Arnold of Villanova* <note>original: "Arnaldo de Villa noua," a famous 13th-century physician and alchemist</note>, who wrote about the fire of coals; for he will deceive you by this very thing. *Almadir* says that only the invisible rays of our fire are sufficient. Another brings as an example the celestial heat, which by its reflections works toward the coagulation <note>the process of turning a liquid substance into a solid or "fixed" state</note> and perfection of **mercury**, as well as toward metallic generation, by its continuous motion. Again the same says: make a vaporous fire, digesting as if cooking, continuous, yet not flying or boiling, enclosed, surrounded by air, not burning but altering and penetrating. Truly I have now spoken; if you are a true philosopher, you will well understand every mode of fire and of exciting heat. Thus he spoke. *Salmanazar* says: Our fire is a corrosive fire, which draws the air like a cloud over our vessel, in which cloud the rays of this fire are hidden. If this dew of chaos <note>original: "rore cahos"; in alchemy, "chaos" often refers to the prime matter or the initial state of the substance</note> and humidity of the cloud are lacking, an error has been committed. Again *Almadir* says: unless the fire warms our **sun** <note>here referring to gold or the masculine principle in the work</note> with its moisture, through the "dung of the mountain" <note>original: "fimum montis," a term for a source of gentle, steady heat, often metaphorically associated with fermenting organic matter</note> by a tempered ascent, we shall not be partakers of the **white** or the **red stone** <note>the White and Red Elixirs, the goals of the alchemical process</note>. All these things clearly demonstrate to us the hidden fire of the wise. In summary, this is the matter of our fire: namely, that it be kindled by the quiet spirit of sensible fire, which pushes upwards like heated chaos, from the opposite side and above our philosophical matter, which heat

### Chapter Twenty.
### Concerning the ferment of the philosophers, and the weight.

The philosophers have labored a great deal in the art of ferments <term>ferment: in alchemy, a substance added to a base metal to "leaven" or transform it into gold or silver, much like yeast in bread</term> and fermentations, which seems the most important among other arts. Concerning this, some have even made a vow to God and to other philosophers that they would never reveal the manifest secret of this matter, not even through similarities or parables. Since, however, Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure regarded as the father of alchemy</note>, the father of all philosophers, in the book of his seven treatises, reveals the ferments most clearly, saying they consist of nothing but their own paste. He further states that the ferment whitens the preparation, prevents burning, and utterly holds back the flow of the tincture <note>the "tincture" is the liquid form of the Philosopher's Stone capable of staining or transforming metals</note>, comforts the bodies, and increases their union. He also says that this is the key and the end of the work, concluding that the ferment is nothing else but a paste: such as that of the sun <note>original: "solis"—the alchemical symbol for gold</note>, which is nothing but the sun; and that of the moon <note>original: "lune"—the alchemical symbol for silver</note>, which is nothing other than the moon. Others affirm that the ferment is the soul, which, unless it is rightly prepared through the mastery <note>magisterium: the specialized process of creating the Stone</note>, achieves nothing. Some zealots of this art seek the art in common sulfur, arsenic, tutty <note>zinc oxide</note>, orpiment <note>arsenic trisulfide, used as a yellow pigment</note>, vitriol, etc., but in vain. Because the substance which is sought is the same as that from which it must be drawn. No—

<meta>This page continues a discussion on alchemical fermentation and transitions into a detailed explanation of "weight" (proportions) in the Great Work. It follows a previous discussion on the results of the fermentation process.</meta>

->54 AURORA<-

...it must be noted <note>The Latin text starts with "zandum," likely the conclusion of "notandum" (it must be noted) from the previous page.</note>, therefore, that fermentations of this kind, according to the wishes of the zealous, do not succeed as they would wish, but—as is clear from what was said above—only through natural successes. To come at last to the matter of weight, this must be observed in two ways: the first is natural, and the second is artificial.

The natural weight achieves its effect in the earth, through nature and "concordance" (harmony), of which Arnaldus <note>Likely Arnaldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311), a celebrated medieval physician and alchemist.</note> speaks. If more or less is added to the earth than nature can endure, the soul is suffocated, no fruit is perceived, and no fixation <term>fixation: the process of stabilizing a volatile substance so that it can endure fire without evaporating</term> occurs. The same must be judged regarding water; if more or less of it is taken, it will bring no less an inconvenient harm: an excess of it makes the matter moist beyond measure, while a deficiency makes it drier and harder than is right. If more air is present, the tincture <term>tincture: the transformative essence or "spirit" capable of changing the color and quality of a metal</term> is imprinted too strongly; if too little, the body will turn out pale. Likewise, if the fire is too fierce, the matter is scorched; if too weak, it has no power for drying, nor for dissolving, nor for heating the other elements: in these things the elemental weight consists.

But the artificial weight is most hidden, since it is enclosed within the magical art of weighings. Between the spirit, soul, and body, the philosophers say that the weight consists of sulfur, acting as the ruler of the work; for indeed the soul desires sulfur above all else, and necessarily observes it by reason of weight. Understand it in this manner: our matter is united to red sulfur...
