It must be understood that our spagyric <term>Spagyric: An alchemical method of preparation involving the separation of a substance into its components and their subsequent purification and recombination</term> and anatomical little man <term>Homunculus: Literally "little man," referring here to a miniature representation of the human form or a being "created" within an alchemical vessel</term> is enclosed within our vessel. 138 ### ANATOMIA <note>original: "ANATOMIA"; while this usually means the study of the human body, here it refers to the structural "dissection" or detailed description of a chemical furnace.</note>

...so that the height of its body is **twenty-four inches** <note>original: "pollicum"; a historical unit of measurement roughly equal to a modern inch, based on the width of a thumb.</note> on the inside. It is to be divided into **three chambers**, each of which should be eight inches high and twenty-four wide, and it should have a **triple base**, each one perforated in a different manner.

The lowest base should gape open with a hole such that its radius <note>original: "semidiametro"</note> occupies a fourth part of the area of its own base. The middle base should be perforated with **six openings** at equal distances, so that the interval between one and another is equal to the width of the open holes. The diameter of these holes will be one-fourth of the diameter of the large hole in the lowest base.

By this geometric reasoning, it will be found that the six upper holes have a <term>sesquialteral ratio</term> <note>A ratio of 3:2, where one value is 1.5 times the other.</note> to the lowest one. From this, it is gathered that:
*   **Fire of the first degree** will be three-eighths of the linear measure, corresponding to nine inches of the same;
*   **Fire of the second degree** will be three-fourths, or eighteen inches;
*   **Fire of the third degree** of fire will be twenty-seven inches;
*   And **fire of the fourth degree** will be thirty-six inches.

These similarly have a <term>supertriple ratio</term> <note>A ratio of 4:1.</note> to the twelve inches of the lowest diameter, and a **sesquialteral ratio** to the linear measure, just as the upper holes are opened in proportion to the lowest. Seek another proportion if you wish, such as equating four upper holes to the lower one, where the radius of the lower results in the full diameter of each of the upper... <note>The text ends mid-sentence; "effi-" is likely the start of "efficit," meaning "makes" or "results in."</note>

->VIVORVM 139<-

...achieve this: so that when the upper <margin><unclear>illegible marks</unclear></margin> parts are completely open, their opening is equal to that of the lower part. It should be noted, furthermore, that the middle base of this kind of furnace ought to be double, with the upper part being rotatable by its own handle. By this means, the upper vents can be opened or closed step-by-step—by a quarter part, a half, three-quarters, or entirely—and by that regulation, the heat of the fire can be increased or decreased whenever it is necessary.

The <term>cupel</term><note>Latin: *capella*. Furthermore, a side tower should be filled with charcoal and closed at the top; it should have a vent into the furnace through which the ignited coals may stimulate the fire, as you will see is necessary according to your own experience.

...so that it does not breathe <note>The author advises keeping the sample sealed to prevent the escape of "vital spirits" or vapors</note>: one must refrain from using vessels made of other materials, lest the urine take on some external quality from them. After the physician has received it, he should bring the vessel to the fire, and it should remain closed so that it regains the same heat it had at the time it was emitted from the natural vessel <note>original: "vaſe naturali," referring to the bladder</note>. Finally, it should be agitated by shaking and thoroughly mixed with its own **sediment** <term>fecibus: literally "dregs" or "lees," referring to the solid matter that settles at the bottom of a urine sample</term>. Once this is done, let it rest, always with the vessel closed, so that the more subtle spirits do not perhaps escape because of the movements. 

Afterward, the vessel should be opened when the urine has settled, and it should be poured along with its sediment into a tin vessel up to the brim, so that a judgment may first be made based on its weight. If it happens that the urine weighs exactly eight ounces, it is a good omen, indicating that the disease is very curable; the closer it is to this weight, the better the outlook. 

Likewise, the less it weighs compared to the assigned weight, the more the disease must be judged to approach **sulfur**; and the more it weighs above the aforementioned eight ounces, the more the disease must be declared **mercurial** <note>In the "Tria Prima" theory of Paracelsus, all diseases were thought to stem from imbalances in three fundamental principles: Sulfur (combustibility), Mercury (fluidity/volatility), and Salt (solidity/weight)</note>. However, when it weighs significantly less than eight ounces, approaching seven or even lower, it should be thought that the disease arises from **salt**, because this is lighter than the other two primary principles.

If you observe the urine to be turbid and thick, it will be judged with certainty that the disease has fixed its root in the blood and in the body, and this all the more violently...

## On colors.
<note>original: *De coloribus*</note>

Younger persons produce urine that is thicker, in the manner of the whey of milk flowing from cheeses, which is poorly digested and imperfect.

Those of middle age have urine that is very thin, yellow, excellently digested, and tinted with a most beautiful color.

### On the humors that rise during distillation

The glass vessel containing the urine is to be placed into a copper bowl <term>sand bath; a "cupella" or container filled with sand used to provide even, indirect heat from a furnace</term> filled with sand, situated at the top of the furnace. It should be submerged in the sand by three twenty-fourth parts of a linear measure—or three inches—so that twenty-one inches remain above. 

Before the fire is lit in the side-furnace, a large sheet of paper should be prepared, which has been folded into twenty-four equal parts along its length, and only four parts across its width. On the right side of the paper, all the signs that appear on the right side of the vessel are to be recorded; likewise, those that appear on the left are to be recorded on the left. In the middle spaces of the paper between the left and right, those things which are seen from the front of the vessel and from the back should be noted: specifically, in the space closest to the right, note what is manifest on the front part of the vessel, and in the other middle space, what is on the back. 

It must be noted that the beak of the <term>helm; the "galea" or "alembic," the cap of a still where vapors collect and are directed out through a spout</term> should be placed face-to-face, turned toward the examiner. That side shall be considered the "front" part of the vessel, and it corresponds to the same part of the body being examined <note>The author is suggesting a "microcosm" theory where the distillation vessel mirrors the human torso.</note>.

### The first degree of fire, and the indicators of the liquor of Mercury.

**W**hen the sweat of the urine adheres to some part of the vessel in the form of drops, it provides a most certain sign of excessive moisture in that same part of the patient’s body, which excites pain; and the larger the drops are, the greater the pain; likewise, from smaller drops, lesser pains are to be judged.

If the drops remain of the same size without variation, let it be a sign that the disease is **fixed** <note>meaning the illness is chronic or firmly localized in that specific organ</note> in that part of the body which is indicated.

When the color of the drops has changed from white to lemon-yellow <note>original: "citrinum"; a standard term in uroscopy to describe a specific yellow hue associated with certain bodily states.</note>, it will be a sign that the first and weak liquids, having been separated from the urine, have ascended, and only the thinner vapors mixed with the "earth" <note>This refers to the solid particles or sediment remaining in the liquid.</note> have remained in the cucurbit <term>cucurbit: a gourd-shaped glass vessel used as the boiling flask in a distillation setup</term>.

In this second receiver, the entire vapor is to be collected, so that in the bottom of the flask nothing is left except the driest dregs. This can be easily noticed because the flask is no longer submerged so deeply in the sand <note>Refers to a "sand bath," where a vessel is placed in heated sand to ensure gentle, even distribution of heat.</note>. 

Everything that appeared from the first removal of the receiver until the second must be noted quite accurately on paper, because the first and most important signs of this art <note>The "art" of chemistry or medicine.</note> lie hidden there. Finally, both liquids that were collected should be weighed separately, and the weight of each should be noted along with the other signs. Indeed, it is easiest to judge by weight which substance has the most power over the primary elements in the urine and in the patient’s body. 

For example: let us suppose the raw urine weighs eight ounces and fifteen grains. Likewise, the liquid from the first distillation weighs five ounces and two hundred and fifty grains; the liquid from the second distillation (from the vapor) weighs one ounce and two hundred and fifty grains. These added together equal seven ounces and ten grains <note>Early modern weight systems often used 480 grains to the ounce. The author's math here implies a slight loss of material during the process or a specific local measurement system.</note>. If these are subtracted from the eight ounces and fifteen grains of the original sample, only one ounce and five grains [remain]...

When, therefore, the lemon-yellow <note>original: "citrinus"</note> color of the drops is seen to change into something thicker and redder, it must be changed, and <margin><unclear>faint, illegible note</unclear></margin>

...is to be moved according to the receiving vessel, after a third has been applied. In this operation, one must note with the greatest diligence the "adhesions" <note>In early chemistry/alchemy, this refers to how substances or residues stick to the sides of the glass during distillation.</note> of both vapors and smokes, as well as oily and saline materials. First, note the places from which they rise and take their origin. Second, note what they will adhere to, and meanwhile through what places they pass while ascending—that is, between their beginning and their end or final position. Third, note what their condition, state, adhesion, and form will be, and whether they increase, decrease, or vanish.

But because sulfur <note>original: "sulphur"; in the 16th and 17th centuries, sulfur was considered one of the three primary alchemical principles (Tria Prima), representing the soul, inflammability, and the vital heat of the body.</note>—which is mentioned in this place—holds dominion especially over the blood, reason demands that we observe the distinctions of blood according to the diversity of persons, regarding both their age and their physical qualities.

In children, the blood is considered thin, sharp, and full of a watery substance.

In adults, such as those of thirty years, it is pure, clean, subtle <note>In historical medical texts, "subtle" refers to a substance that is refined, highly active, or capable of penetrating deep into the tissues.</note>, and hot.

In the elderly, however, it is scarce, dense, and thick.

In the obese, it is also scarce, heavy, cold, and thin.

In the lean, on the contrary, it is abundant, hot, and smooth.

In men, moreover, it is much thinner and hotter than in women, in whom it is dense, thick, and cold.

All blood that is abundant and thick provides men with strength, boldness, and vigor.

158               ->ANATOMY<-

Conversely, a more subtle [spirit] exhibits timidity, fear, and even stupor. 

It also possesses certain specific conditions in each individual member: for instance, it is extremely subtle in the pulsating veins <term>pulsating veins: the arteries, which in early medicine were thought to carry vital spirits</term>, pure in the lung, hot in the heart, thick in the more common veins, mixed in the liver, but cold in the spleen. It should be noted that if its virtues and faculties are hindered by obstructions <note>original: "opilationibus," a common medical term for blockages in the vessels or pores</note> or dissipations, the life of any kind of animal is likewise diminished by this very fact. This is especially true in humans, in whom it is not only the dwelling place of the soul, but also the fuel <note>original: "fomentum"</note> of their spirit and the greatest restoration <note>original: "refocillatio," meaning a reviving or warming back to life</note> of all parts of their body; however, in irrational beasts, it is the nourishment of all flesh. 

Now at last, we must proceed to the fire of this third degree <note>In alchemy and early chemistry, "degrees" of fire referred to specific levels of heat intensity</note> by opening the air-holes to three-quarters of their capacity, once the movable base has been moved forward. With this heat gradually increased, spirits will arise along with certain thicker, oily, and sulfurous substances, which will indeed be resolved in three ways: 

**First**, into airy, invisible, and imperceptible moisture <note>original: "verositates," referring to the watery or serum-like vapors released during heating</note>. 

**Second**, in the manner of visible and distinctly perceptible vapors, and toward the end in the form of clouds. 

**Third**, substantially and essentially in the form of a certain oil; likewise bodily in the form of salt, and sensibly in the form of tartar, adhering in a tangible and visible way.

First, therefore, in this third distillation, invisible winds ascend; second, visible fumes, which prefigure for us the most subtle substance of the <term>radical humor|The essential life-sustaining moisture believed to exist in all living beings.</term>, and are of the nature of mercurial and animal water. The beginning, middle, and end of these winds must be most accurately noted, as well as the place where that oily, sulfurous, and spiritual smoke begins to rise. Finally, however, clouds rise from the earthly matter, which are of a saline substance and a corporeal <note>meaning "physical" or "solid"</note> nature. 

For just as it is known that the element of air enters and penetrates the earth through pores and caverns—where, being resolved into water, it always seeks to flow to lower places—similarly in the human body, vapors rising from the stomach penetrate the region of the head. There, finally resolved through <term>condensation|The text uses "congelationem," which in early science referred to the thickening of vapors into liquids or solids.</term>, they flow down to the lower members of the body, which they sometimes miserably torment.

**Second**, Tartar remains fixed and heavy; the "Mummy" is reddish and light; moreover the slime, or Mercury, is more volatile <note>original: "flatiùs"; literally "airier" or more prone to evaporating</note>, burning, and not fixed.

Likewise, from the material dissolved by the previously mentioned washing from the dregs, it can be done as above: by weighing first, and afterwards burning (beyond the point of glowing heat), and then when it has cooled, weighing it again. That which is lost in weight must be judged to have been the Mercury within it.

Let this be its final test: namely, that it now be burned a second time with a fire more violent than before. If any weight is missing after the material has cooled, that very thing is judged to have been Sulfur. What remains after these burnings is the Salt of that same urine.

Let each of these things be noted quite accurately and recorded.

->**Signs of Colors.**<-

In order to better judge colors in this art, the colors are to be borrowed from the first three natural principles <note>This refers to the *Tria Prima* of alchemy: Sulfur, Mercury, and Salt, which were believed to be the base components of all matter.</note> of precious stones, distributed according to their own nature.

->**Judgments taken from the colors of sulfurous precious stones in the examination of urines.**<-

The color of **Citrine** stone indicates a defect of the nutritive and expulsive faculties in the examination of urines. <note>In Galenic medicine, these "faculties" were the body's natural abilities to absorb nutrients and expel waste.</note>

The color of **Topaz** [indicates] an abscess in the kidneys.

The color of **Chrysolite** [indicates] an increase of years. <note>original: "annorum augmentum"; likely referring to conditions associated with advanced age or the aging process as reflected in the body's chemistry.</note>

The color of **Emerald** [indicates] an obstruction <term>opilation: a historical medical term for a blockage or clogging of the internal vessels or "pores" of the body</term> of the radical humors. <note>The "radical humors" or "radical moisture" were thought to be the essential life-sustaining fluids of the body.</note>

The color of **Prasine** <note>A leek-green gemstone, often a type of chalcedony.</note> [indicates] damage to the kidneys and the diaphragm.

The color of **Malachite** [indicates] a defect of the concoctions. <note>In early medicine, "concoction" referred to the various stages of digestion and metabolic transformation of food into blood and humors.</note>

The color of **Jasper** [indicates] a contraction of the pulsating veins <note>In this period, "pulsating veins" referred to the arteries, which were distinguished from non-pulsating veins.</note> and the limbs.

The color of **Turquoise** [indicates] a hindrance of the natural powers and of reason.

->**Mercurial and animal colors of stones.**<-

The color of **Crystal** indicates a lessening of the digestive and attractive faculties.

The color of **Beryl** [indicates] a swelling of the flesh, of the stomach, and of the intestines.

The **Lung** is the receptacle for the element of air, serving as the sustenance and nourishment of life. This organ opens and compresses like a pair of bellows; by this natural mechanism, it takes in air and releases it, always drawing in new air. By breathing this toward the heart to cool it, the lung continuously tempers the intense heat hidden in the heart's center with fresh nourishment. Without this respiration, that <term>fiery vigor</term> <note>original: "igneus vigor," referring to the "vital spark" or innate heat believed to sustain life in Galenic medicine.</note> of life by which all animals live would be suffocated—no differently than we see that an artificial fire cannot exist at all without air.

The **Liver** is the receptacle for the purer parts of nourishment, which are separated by the stomach and sent to the liver so that they may be transformed into blood. From this blood, all the other members of the body subsequently take their food. In this organ, one must note a most excellent natural separation of three parts: one is the purer and nobler blood, taken up for the nourishment of the body; this is separated from a second kind of impure and superfluous blood, which is full of water and excess moisture. The third part is a <term>poisonous bitterness</term> <note>original: "amaritudo venenosa," referring to yellow bile or gall.</note>, which is naturally separated from the others so that it does not mix with the nutrients.

The **Heart** is the source of the <term>vegetative life</term> <note>In traditional philosophy, the "vegetative soul" governed basic growth and nutrition.</note>, as well as the center from which the natural and radical <note>The "radical moisture" and "natural heat" were considered the two essential components of the life force.</note> heat proceeds—

<summary>This page describes the physiological functions of the lungs as a cooling bellows for the heart's heat and the liver as a refinery that separates nutrients into blood, waste water, and bile.</summary>

...the <term>radical moisture</term><note>Latin: *radicalis humor*. In pre-modern medicine, this was thought to be the fundamental life-sustaining fluid found in all living things, similar to the oil in a lamp.</note> into any other members; and it is the dwelling place of all emotions, both good and bad. It should certainly be considered how, through fear and dread, the <term>attractive faculty</term><note>The "attractive faculty" was the heart's supposed power to pull substances toward itself, a key concept in Galenic medicine before the modern understanding of blood circulation.</note> in the heart draws blood to itself from other members of the body so effectively, and sometimes recalls it so suddenly, that it can lead to a flooding and even the heart’s own suffocation due to the excessive abundance of blood. On the other hand, because of excessive joy and sudden, unexpected gladness, its <term>expulsive power</term><note>The counterpart to the attractive faculty; the heart's ability to push blood and "spirit" outward.</note> drives the blood away from itself toward the other members, so that it may make them participants in its joy. This is a sign of its goodness and its great bond of fellowship with the rest of the members. Conversely, through hatred, or envy, and especially through disordered anger, the <term>vicious fluids</term><note>Latin: *vitiosi liquores*. These are "corrupted" humors—yellow bile, black bile, phlegm, or blood—that were believed to cause disease when out of balance.</note> mixed with the blood are drawn to the heart as a refuge, coming not only from the <term>gall</term><note>Latin: *felle*. Referring to the gallbladder or yellow bile.</note> but indeed from all other members. All the moisture retreats with it, leaving the limbs destitute of their strength, so that the <term>vital spirits</term><note>Latin: *vitales spiritus*. A refined substance thought to be carried in the blood to provide heat and life to the body.</note> fail in them likewise, and the <term>animal spirits</term><note>Latin: *animales [spiritus]*. The most refined spirits, located in the brain and heart, responsible for thought and sensation.</note> of the heart are infected by them.

The **Stomach** is the first receptacle of foods and drinks, and the first separator of the pure from the impure; it sends the pure to the **Liver** and the impure to the intestines. It is situated precisely under the diaphragm, just like a pot under its lid.

    The Diaphragm, like a dividing wall <note>original: "firmamentum"</note>, acts as a separation and middle ground between the upper cavity (in which the lungs, heart, and arterial veins are located) and the lower one, containing the stomach, liver, gallbladder, spleen, intestines, kidneys, bladder, and reproductive vessels: in all of which, generally, the nutritive faculties of the body reside.

    The Bladder is the receptacle and cleansing-drain <term>emunctorium: an organ or duct that carries off waste</term> for useless water, separated from the nutritional blood so that the body may be evacuated of it.

# Indications of head ailments, under the examination of urine.

**Three** are the most powerful ailments and diseases of the head: **Headache** <note>original: *Cephalalgia*</note>, **Migraine** <note>original: *Haemicrania*</note>, and **Chronic Headache** <note>original: *Cephalea*; usually refers to a persistent, intense, or long-term condition</note>. The first takes its origin from purulent discharges <note>original: *saniosis fluxionibus*</note>; the second from "false" humors <note>unbalanced or corrupted bodily fluids</note>; the third, truly, from glass-like fluids <note>original: *vitreis liquoribus*; likely referring to clear, viscous phlegm</note>.

### On General Headache <note>original: *De Cephalalgia*</note>.

The first indications of this disease are these:

**First**, certain tiny droplets, like coagulated sweat, appear very subtly in the uppermost region of the vessel <note>the 'head' or top of the glass urinal</note>, namely the head, immediately after the disappearance of the "cloud" <note>the *nubecula*, a suspension often seen in urine samples</note> under the first degree of heat. These are of three types: **red**, which denote dim-sighted ailments of the eyes; **thick and heavy**, which signify watery eyes <term>epiphora: an overflow of tears caused by blockage or excessive secretion</term>; and **turbulent**, which signify bleary-eyed inflammation.

**Secondly**, droplets of this kind swell up like little blisters <note>original: *vesicularum*</note>, and afterwards are compressed by their own weight and take the form of millet seeds under the second degree of heat. At this stage, a judgment of three diseases can also be made from three colors: from a **transparent whiteness**, catarrh <note>a buildup of mucus in the nose or throat</note>; from a **dim whiteness**, gas in the ears; and from a **viscous whiteness**, a blockage of the nostrils.

**Thirdly**, the grains which have been mentioned are dissolved by the heat of the fire into a substance like greasy fat, which flows down the sides of the vessel.

Finally, by a more vehement heat of fire, it is separated from the vessel under the third degree of fire. A muddy-yellow <note>original: "luteo"</note> color signifies <term>Ephialtes</term> <note>the "nightmare," historically viewed as a physical obstruction of the breath or spirit during sleep</note>; by lightness or levity, it signifies <term>Epilepsy</term>. If, however, the said matter is compact and strong, it denotes **Mania** <note>a state of frenzied madness</note> and **Apoplexy** <note>a sudden loss of consciousness or a stroke</note>.

## On Chronic Headache
<note>original: "De Cephalæa". In early modern medicine, "Cephalaea" referred to persistent, chronic, or intense headaches as opposed to "Cephalalgia," which was a more general term.</note>

In this location, the drops appear much larger than those preceding, and are turbulent from the start, just like the very matter from which they took their origin. Then, having become clearer, they take on various colors. They are fixed in the **first degree of fire** <note>The "first degree" typically refers to a gentle heat, such as that of a lamp or warm ashes.</note>. Their **citrine** <note>lemon-yellow</note> color denotes **Coryza** <note>original: "Corizam"; a common cold or inflammation of the mucous membranes in the nose</note>: a bright citrine signifies a **polyp** in the nostrils.

**Secondly**, some of these drops diminish and disappear, but other colorations appear and remain until the **final degree of fire**, in which they eventually vanish. By their **green** color, they signify **contortions** <note>likely muscular spasms or cramps</note>; by **dark vapors** <note>original: "flatibus"</note>, they signify **lethargy**; and by a **green color** combined with **acidity**, they denote **tetanus** <note>In this historical context, "tetanus" refers generally to rigid muscle tension or "stretching" diseases rather than the modern bacterial infection.</note>.

**Thirdly**, in the same position as the drops, a certain colored matter is fixed, fatty like oil, which afterwards, when dried, leaves traces of **transparent salt** of the color natural to its own essence, which is finally dissolved in the **third degree of fire**. By a **blue** color, **worms** are denoted; if this color should be very bright, it signifies a deprivation of rest and sleep. **Bitterness** <note>original: "Amarulentia"</note> indeed signifies all manner of restlessness.

In the final examinations of **Chronic Headache** <note>original: "Cephalalgia"</note>, it must be known that **catarrhs** produce a **pale flame** mixed with a **flesh color**, and a **yellowish smoke**, dark and turbulent.

In **Migraine** <note>original: "Hemicrania"</note>, the **tartars** <note>term: Tartar; In the medical theory of Paracelsus, "tartar" refers to stony or metabolic waste deposits in the body that cause disease by obstructing organs.</note>, as well as **salty liquids**, produce a **yellow flame**, turbid and dark...

->**Signs of the Lungs**<-

The lung is subject primarily to three diseases: namely, inflammation, putrefaction <note>the decay or rotting of tissue</note>, and obstruction <note>original: *opilationi*</note>. The drops of inflammation, at the first grade of fire <note>referring to the gentle heat level of a laboratory furnace</note>, immediately rise up because they are imbued with an airy property; they are of a most beautiful transparency. They often strain through the sides of the glass, reaching into the "first region of the lung" <note>referring to a specific area within the distillation vessel corresponding to the organ</note>, and a moment later, they ascend again, and finally they acquire a viscosity <note>a thick, sticky quality</note>. 

They produce three primary colors, depending on the conditions: from heat and thinness, they produce a white color and a spongy, viscous form, by which they signify coughing and the spitting of blood <note>original: *amopthyfim*, referring to hemoptysis</note>. From mucilaginous matter, they produce a waxy color and a foul odor, and from this, they indicate pleurisy <note>inflammation of the membranes surrounding the lungs</note>. 

Putrefaction of the lung makes itself known by its own signs at the second grade of fire; specifically, that in place of drops, a fatty and colored <term>mumia</term> <note>in Paracelsian medicine, this refers to the vital essence or a balsamic substance extracted from the body</note> is manifested around the region of the lung. In the place where the putrefaction itself lies hidden, it appears through a dark and turbid smoke, which finally turns into a flesh-like color. The lung is inflated for three reasons: first, due to a deficiency, from which it contracts a harshness and thickness, and by this it signifies atrophy <note>the wasting away of the organ</note>; second...

From "accidents" <note>in this context, external symptoms or secondary conditions</note> it takes other colors, such as from inflammation, a milky white and turbid color, which denotes dyspnea <note>shortness of breath</note> to us. From tartar, it takes a gypsum-like color, through which it indicates a cough. But from obstruction, it becomes yellow and thick; from this, it signifies orthopnea <note>difficulty breathing while lying down</note> and asthma.

### Colors of the Lung

The color of the lung due to inflammation is lemon-yellow mixed with the redness of a Pallas stone <note>original: "Pallasij lapidis"; likely a reference to a specific mineral or gemstone described in historical lapidaries, possibly a variety of golden or reddish chrysolite</note> and blue. The color of putrefaction in the lung is black, thick, and turbulent, manifest in a vaporous smoke; and a shining film is spread over the burnt matter.

Obstruction gives off a smoke that soon passes, not without whistling and noise. The burnt matter of this condition produces three colors: first, a white color, like gypsum or chalk; second, a brick-red or reddish color; and third, an ashen color.

->**Signs of the Heart through Examination**<-

The vital spirits in the heart have three principal faculties: namely, the **Attractive** and magnetic; the **Retentive** or sustaining; and thirdly, the **Expulsive**.

The **attractive virtue** has three colors corresponding to the blood. For instance, blood that is lemon-colored <note>original: "citrinus"</note>, transparent, clear and bright, and not acidic, indicates a spasm. Red, fatty, succulent, and sharp blood denotes a burning fever <term>causon: a term derived from Greek medicine referring to an acute, inflammatory fever characterized by intense heat and thirst</term>. But that blood which has a foul-smelling and mucilaginous juice signifies sorrow and sadness of the heart, as well as a continuous fever <term>synocha: a type of fever that persists at a high intensity without breaks or remissions</term>.

The **retentive virtue** likewise has three colors through which it indicates "tartar" <note>In the medical theories of the time, "tartar" referred to the stony, hardening deposits formed by metabolic processes, believed to cause various obstructions and pains in the organs.</note>. The first is in the manner of viscous water—tenacious, non-flowing, white, and clear—which signifies stone formation <term>lithodomia: a term related to the development of stones or calculi within the body</term>. A similarity in color and matter that is hardened, of a milky white and cloudy color, indicates palpitations of the heart. An ash-colored, hard, and non-flowing matter, resembling gypsum or quicklime, denotes syncope <note>A sudden loss of consciousness or fainting spells.</note>.

## Signs of the Liver, its Diseases, and Symptoms

**S**pirits of nature <note>original: "Spiritus naturales"; in traditional medicine, these were the "natural spirits" responsible for nutrition and metabolism, believed to be produced in the liver</note> in the liver likewise possess three faculties: namely, the **dissolutive** (dissolving), the **transmutative** (transforming), and the **subtilizing** (refining).

The **dissolutive faculty** is particularly prone to heat and displays two colors. First, a flesh-colored <note>original: "caneoli"; likely referring to a pale, reed-like or pinkish-white hue</note> hue, which, through its fresh **mumia** <note>In this context, *mumia* refers to the Paracelsian concept of a vital life-force or "internal balsam" inherent in living tissues, not an Egyptian mummy</note>, foreshadows **anasarca** <note>A condition of severe, generalized swelling of the whole body</note>. Second, a brownish-yellow color accompanied by its watery mumia denotes **ascites** <note>excess fluid accumulation in the abdominal cavity</note>, **tympanites** <note>swelling of the abdomen due to trapped gas</note>, and **yellow jaundice**.

The **transmutative virtue** is subject to obstructions. Through its date-brown color and a putrid mumia, it signifies **hectic fever**; whereas a congealed mumia denotes **obstruction**, **scirrhus** <note>original: "scyrrum"; a hard, firm tumor or hardening of the organ tissue</note>, and **black jaundice**.

The **subtilizing faculty**, however, is subject to corruption and the putrefaction of the mumia. This is recognized by a dark yellow color resembling **amber** <note>original: "electri"</note>, from which one may judge an **abscess** <note>original: "apostematio"</note> and the corruption of some vein in the li- <note>The text breaks off here mid-word: "hæpate" (liver)</note>

## **Signs of the Spleen.**

The spleen likewise possesses three natural faculties: one consuming, and of a spongy matter; another attractive, moist, and not solid; a third of an insatiable, earthy, and thick nature. The **consuming power**, by its color in the manner of thin whitish water, through a leaden yellowness, and through <term>mumia<note>original: "mumia"; in early modern medicine, this referred to a resinous or bituminous substance believed to have healing properties, sometimes associated with embalmed bodies or mineral pitch.</note></term>, together with crystal bubbles, denotes blockage and piercing <note>Likely referring to sharp, stabbing pains in the side.</note>. The **attractive faculty**, appearing under a lemon-yellow color—transparent, clear, fluid, thin, and mucilaginous—indicates superfluity and bloating. The **insatiable faculty**, through purulent matter that is somewhat clear, thin, and having a transparent color like <term>manna<note>original: "manna"; a reference to the pale, semi-transparent color and consistency of plant-derived medicinal resins.</note></term>, signifies hardness, <term>scirrhus<note>original: "scyrrum"; a hard, dense tumor or hardening of the organ tissue.</note></term>, phantasms, fainting fits, sadness, very heavy dreams, and also <term>erysipelas<note>original: "heresipelā"; a painful bacterial skin infection characterized by large, red, inflamed patches.</note></term>.

## **Signs of the Kidneys and the Bladder.**

The **attractive faculty**, under the color of cloudy milk and under a dark yellowness, with a woolly and shaggy form, denotes—due to the destruction of <term>diamne<note>original: "diamne"; a rare technical term in Paracelsian medicine, likely referring to a vital salt or substance that maintains the integrity of the urinary system.</note></term>—abscesses, ulcerations, and in men, from the aforementioned signs, <term>gonorrhea<note>In this historical context, "gonorrhea" referred generally to any involuntary discharge of fluid or mucus from the genitals, not strictly the modern infection.</note></term>, <term>buboes<note>Swollen lymph nodes in the groin.</note></term>, <term>priapism<note>A persistent and often painful erection.</note></term>, and impotence in sexual intercourse; but in women, menstrual flows both red and white <note>Likely referring to regular menstruation and "the whites" or leukorrhea.</note>, and defects of the womb. ### Conclusion.

So that we may finally bring an end to this **Anatomy of Living Bodies**, <note>The "Anatomy of the Living" (Anatomia Vivorum) was a core Paracelsian concept. Unlike traditional anatomy which dissected dead corpses, this "anatomy" sought to understand the living chemical and spiritual forces (the "Archeus") that governed health.</note> which was revealed through a special gift of the Holy Spirit to **Theophrastus Paracelsus**, our teacher of blessed memory. This was done so that the enormous errors of the pagans <note>original: "Ethnicorum". This refers to ancient Greek and Roman authorities like Galen and Avicenna, whose medical systems Paracelsus sought to replace with a Christian, chemically-based medicine.</note> introduced into medicine might not only be corrected, but truly and fundamentally overturned and uprooted. Indeed, these intolerable abuses were brought forth from the underworld by the enemies of God and nature, and should not be permitted anywhere among the nations. We wish to advise good and pious physicians to diligently practice this anatomical art which we propose to them, not merely focusing on these examples written here. For these are provided only as a starting point, so that better...

->## THE ANATOMY OF THEOPHRASTUS PARACELSUS ##<-

From its true origin, Anatomy has been so pushed aside that it has been thought until now that everything pertaining to it can be abundantly understood from a dead man—or rather, a corpse. I clearly value this as very little, indeed, as nothing at all; this is true even though there are still many who value it highly and try to gather many insights from it. Because of this practice, Medicine has been reduced to mere <term>Theory|Theoretica — abstract knowledge or speculation often divorced from the living reality of the patient</term>, and has been supported by great errors up to this point. 

For certain reasons acknowledge this very fact: namely, that things much different than a dead body must be "anatomized" <note>In Paracelsus's view, "anatomy" was not just the dissection of flesh, but the investigation of the inner chemical and spiritual forces of a substance.</note>. For in this way, since Anatomy ought to make any physician well-instructed, it is necessary that it be two-fold: the one being <term>Local|Localis — pertaining to the physical placement and structure of organs</term>, and the other <term>Essential|Essentialis — pertaining to the "essence," life-force, or chemical spirit of the body</term>. Since, therefore, the Local anatomy must be one part of it, I do not see how it can be truly grasped in a corpse, unless it is preceded by a knowledge of the "external" Anatomy <note>Paracelsus often used "external" to refer to the broader world or "macrocosm," which he believed mirrored the "internal" world of the human body</note>, which the Anatomy of the dead body must only then follow. If there were even the smallest regard for the true medical art within you, you would certainly confess that in a corpse there is neither nature...

Indeed, this argument is directed against those who study only this, and think it quite sufficient to know how the lung or the liver hangs, or in what manner the rest of the organs are situated. Therefore, it is fitting for you to seek an anatomy beyond that which a **corpse** <note>original: cadauer</note> prescribes. For the reason is evident: the anatomy of life shows much more toward another life than if I were to seek in a dead person that which pertains to life. Therefore, anatomy must be observed in this way instead: in what manner diseases spread themselves through it, and which places or regions correspond to their own peculiar diseases, or not. If you grasp the reasoning of this anatomy, you may—not undeservedly—boast that you have in some way enrolled your names in **philosophy**, properly and exactly.

I say this because any accident exists as an **exhausted Anatomy**, even though you may wallow in the subject. You have your teachers to thank for this, since you yourselves cannot find anything better than they did, being devoted only to their words. Therefore, since the accident must cover the subject, where will your <term>fourfold divisions</term> <note>A reference to the four humors (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) which formed the basis of medieval medicine.</note> go? Where will your choler go? Where your humors? Where your qualities? Where even the **six natural things**, and as many **non-naturals**? <note>The "six non-naturals" were external factors in Galenic medicine—such as air, food, sleep, and exercise—that were believed to govern health.</note> Should this be the reasoning of the medical profession? For this reason, I cease to wonder at such a vast phalanx and infinite multitude of the sick everywhere, both in houses and in the streets.

But to finally approach this **exhausted Anatomy**, the highest and principal point of the matter is this: the physical body should in no way be considered regarding the origin of disease; nor should anything be done in the subject, nor disease sought within it. Instead, it is just like an apple: tell me, if you have any idea, where does it grow from? Do you say it comes from the earth? You are wrong. For the earth is the first subject. Or does it come from the wood?

<summary>The author mocks traditional physicians for relying on ancient theories of humors and 'non-naturals' while failing to see that disease should not be sought within the physical body itself. He uses the analogy of an apple growing from a tree to begin explaining the true origin of illness.</summary>
<keywords>Anatomy, Humors, Six Non-Naturals, Philosophy of Medicine, Disease Origins, Aristotelian Subject and Accident</keywords>

<meta>The text appears to be a continuation of a philosophical or medical argument regarding the origins of disease, likely from a Paracelsian or anti-Galenic perspective. The opening "gno?" is a fragment of a word from the preceding page (possibly "signo" or "cognoscere").</meta>

190 ->ANATOMY<-

...[know?] nor is this likely: for it is inherent in the subject of the matter. Truly, you speak of this matter in no other way than if someone, seeing a person sticking their head out of a window, were to say that he was born from the house or from the window. Or because a hen’s chick breaks through the shell with its little beak, will you immediately say that it is procreated from the egg? Certainly, as the saying goes, you are wandering the whole way and by the whole heaven. <note>original: "totoque cœlo erratis." A Latin idiom meaning to be as far from the truth as possible, or completely mistaken.</note> Although I do not doubt you have supporters among the unrefined and those who understand the matter poorly.

But you, excellent Lord Doctors, think it is quite permissible for you to believe and to do everything. Not even Aristotle agrees with me, nor do these men believe me—nor will they ever—who have deceived me so many times, or who still try to do so, and who study only for their own benefit. But only those have faith in me by whom <term>both Anatomies</term> <note>In the context of Renaissance medical reform, this often refers to the "local" anatomy of the corpse and the "essential" or "chemical" anatomy of the living body and its diseases.</note> are distinguished. I would have thought there was more erudition in your libraries than that you should be forced to bring forward ignorant laypeople as witnesses for your cause—persuading yourselves that if someone should come out of a cave, he was immediately procreated right there!

But to finally settle the matter and speak summarily: truly all diseases come into the subject from a foreign source. <note>This reflects the "ontological" view of disease—that a sickness is an external entity that enters the body, rather than just an internal imbalance of fluids.</note> From which it follows that as many apples, as many nuts, and as many other fruits as there are, so many also are the diseases. And yet you are not ashamed to cut these into only four parts, namely: "This is melancholy, this is choler," <note>original: "melancholia... colera." These are two of the "four humors" of traditional Greek medicine, which the author is mocking for being too simplistic.</note> and so on. Consider,

<summary>The author critiques traditional physicians for their simplistic view of disease, arguing that illnesses are diverse external entities rather than mere imbalances of the four humors. He uses metaphors of a chick in an egg and a person in a window to illustrate the folly of confusing a location with a point of origin.</summary>

<meta>This page appears to be the conclusion of a treatise on the relationship between the natural world (the Macrocosm) and human health (the Microcosm), likely written by Paracelsus or one of his followers. It continues the argument that diseases are not caused by the traditional "four humors" but correspond to every element, mineral, and celestial phenomenon found in nature.</meta>

->OF THE LIVING.<-                                 191

...consider <note>The Latin text starts with a fragment "derâte," likely part of "considerate" from the previous page.</note>, the element of fire, from which both snow and heat proceed, both cold and heat. Similarly, for as many occurrences of fiery impressions <note>original: "ignitarum impressionum"; these are celestial or meteorological phenomena like meteors or lightning</note> as there are, there are just as many illnesses in the Microcosm <note>the human body, viewed as a miniature reflection of the entire universe</note>; and however many minerals exist, there are just as many diseases; and for however many types of *thereniabin* <term>thereniabin: a specialized Paracelsian term for "celestial manna" or a medicinal dew that falls from the heavens onto plants</term> there are, there are just as many diseases. 

But because you do not even understand the external world, nor who arranged each thing there, your imagination provides you with a cover for your ignorance—namely, the "four humors" <note>The traditional medical theory of Galen and Hippocrates, which Paracelsus famously rejected, stating that health was governed by the balance of blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile</note>. Ha! How well-instructed you are indeed! You might as well have woven robes of purple rather than such cheap rags, and showed off your patchwork garments in that way—though even then, they would be of no significance.

->T H E  E N D.<-

<summary>This page concludes a polemic against traditional medicine, arguing that the number of diseases corresponds exactly to the number of minerals and celestial phenomena in the universe rather than the four humors. The author mocks established physicians for using outdated theories to hide their ignorance of nature's true mechanisms.</summary>
